xii
ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
The funding for Australia’s participation in TIMSS 2023 was provided jointly by the Australian Government 
and the state and territory governments. All of Australia’s share of the international overheads and half of 
the basic funding for TIMSS within Australia was defrayed by the Australian Government Department of 
Education, while each state and territory government education department provided funding in proportion to 
the size of its student population.
In Australia, TIMSS is managed by a team from the Australian Council for Educational Research (ACER) 
and guided by the International Assessments Joint National Advisory Committee (IAJNAC). ACER wishes 
to thank the IAJNAC members for their continued interest and commitment throughout every phase of 
the project. Their involvement included assisting with the implementation of TIMSS in schools in their 
jurisdiction, reviewing the report and providing valuable information to ensure the continued success of 
TIMSS in Australia.
The undertaking of TIMSS 2023 was a collaborative effort. A national survey such as TIMSS could not be 
successful without the cooperation of the school systems, principals, teachers, parents and, of course, 
the students. A high participation rate of the randomly selected schools and students is essential for the 
assessment to obtain high-quality data. It is thanks to this level of cooperation that Australia was able to 
satisfy the internationally set response criteria fully for TIMSS 2023. ACER gratefully acknowledges the 
assistance of education system officials Australia-wide, and the principals, teachers, parents and students in 
the participating schools who so generously gave their time and support in contributing to the project.
Parts of this report are reproduced and/or modified, with permission, from the TIMSS 2023 international 
results in mathematics and science (2024) report and the TIMSS 2023 assessment frameworks (2021).

xiii
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This is the second of 2 reports that look at the results of TIMSS 2023 and Australia’s performance. This 
report, Volume II, presents the results from the contextual questionnaires, and examines the home, school, 
and classroom contexts in which learning and achievement occur, as well as student attitudes.
Each chapter focuses on different indicators concerning the school community; the school learning 
environment; mathematics and science teacher characteristics; mathematics and science classroom 
learning environments; and, students’ attitudes and beliefs and use of technology. Together, the different 
indicators of student and school life illustrate some of the many key aspects that make up the school 
experience.
What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is an international comparative 
study of student achievement directed by the International Association for the Evaluation of Educational 
Achievement (IEA). TIMSS was first conducted in 1995 and the 2023 assessment formed the eighth cycle, 
providing 28 years of trends in mathematics and science achievement at Year 4 and Year 8. In Australia, 
TIMSS is managed by the Australian Council for Educational Research and is jointly funded by the Australian 
Government and the state and territory governments. TIMSS is a key part of Australia’s National Assessment 
Program.
The goal of TIMSS is to provide comparative information about educational achievement across countries to 
improve teaching and learning in mathematics and science. TIMSS is based on a research model that uses 
the curriculum, within context, as its foundation. TIMSS is designed, broadly, to align with the mathematics 
and science curricula used in the participating education systems and countries, and focuses on 
assessment at Year 4 and Year 8. TIMSS also provides important data about students’ contexts for learning 
mathematics and science based on questionnaires completed by students and their parents, teachers and 
school principals.
The 2023 cycle of TIMSS completed its transition to a digital assessment; this was the first time it was 
offered as a completely online assessment. TIMSS 2023 also expanded its scope to assess environmental 
knowledge and attitudes. This new component provides valuable insights into students’ understanding of 
environmental issues, including climate change, biodiversity, and conservation.

xiv
Executive summary
Who is assessed?
TIMSS assesses a random sample of Year 4 and/or Year 8 students, drawn from a nationally representative 
sample of schools. Their principals and mathematics and science teachers are also asked to complete a 
survey.
In TIMSS 2023, 58 countries participated at Year 4 and 44 countries participated at Year 8. In total, over 
650,000 students participated worldwide.
In Australia, 283 primary schools and 276 secondary schools participated in the data collection for TIMSS 
2023. At least one intact class from the relevant year level was selected, resulting in a sample of 5,470 Year 4 
students and 8,442 Year 8 students.
What is assessed?
In Australia, students who participated in TIMSS 2023 completed an online assessment that contained 
an even distribution of mathematics and science items. Following the assessment, students completed 
a student questionnaire that sought information about home contexts and student characteristics and 
attitudes towards learning mathematics and science.
Teachers, principals and curriculum experts also completed questionnaires, which enabled the collection of 
information about what is intended to be taught and about how it actually is taught in Australian classrooms.
The data from the student, teacher and principal questionnaires are described and analysed in this report.
How are the results reported?
In this report, information is provided about each of the variables for which results are presented, followed by 
the results for Australia as a whole, and the international averages for comparison. Then, for some variables, 
we provide percentages by geographic location and school socioeconomic composition (for school or 
classroom-level variables) or gender or student-level socioeconomic background (for student-level variables)
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, the results from the school questionnaire and teacher 
questionnaire are presented with regard to students. That is, each result is reported as the percentage of 
students who attended a school that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were 
taught by teachers who responded in a particular way.

Executive summary
xv
KEY FINDINGS
Composition of schools and school resources (Chapter 2)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 2 for detailed analysis.
	
z Year 4 and Year 8 students who attended more affluent schools had higher achievement in mathematics 
and science than students at more disadvantaged schools.
	
z Higher percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students from schools in major cities than from schools in 
regional and remote areas were at schools with more affluent student bodies.
	
z At Year 4, 42% of Australian students attended schools where more than 90% of students had English as 
their first language, compared to the international average of 66%. At Year 8, this was 44% of Australian 
students, compared to the international average of 63%.
	
z At Year 4, 48% of students attended schools where less than 25% of students begin school with literacy 
and numeracy skills. Those students recorded average mathematics and science scores that were 
around 30 points lower than those recorded by students at schools where 25–75% of the student 
population had literacy and numeracy skills when they began school.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more disadvantaged schools than from more affluent 
schools where less than 25% of students enter school with literacy and numeracy skills.
	
z 48% of Year 4 students and 35% of Year 8 students attended schools where mathematics instruction 
was somewhat affected by resource shortages, compared to the international averages of 62% 
and 60%.
	
z While there was no significant difference at Year 4, Year 8 students who attended schools where 
mathematics instruction was not affected by resource shortages achieved at a significantly higher level 
in mathematics than students who attended schools where instruction was somewhat affected.
	
z 60% of Year 4 students and 33% of Year 8 students attended schools where science instruction was 
somewhat affected by resource shortages, compared to the international averages of 63% and 60%.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, students who attended schools where science instruction was not affected 
by resource shortages achieved at a significantly higher level in science than students at schools where 
instruction was somewhat affected.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more disadvantaged schools than from more affluent 
schools where instruction was somewhat affected by resource shortages.
	
z At Year 4, 41% of Australian students attended schools with principals who had a postgraduate degree 
in educational leadership, the same as the international average. At Year 8, 51% of Australian students 
attended schools with a principal who had a postgraduate degree in educational leadership, compared 
to the international average of 45%.
	
z There was a higher percentage of Year 8 students from more affluent schools than from more 
disadvantaged schools with principals who had a postgraduate degree in educational leadership, but 
this difference was not seen for Year 4 students.

xvi
Executive summary
School climate, discipline and safety (Chapter 3)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 3 for detailed analysis.
	
z 10% of Year 4 students and 15% of Year 8 students attended schools whose principals reported a very 
high emphasis on academic success in their schools. These students had significantly higher average 
mathematics and science achievement than those from schools whose principals reported a high or 
medium emphasis on academic success.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more affluent schools than from more disadvantaged 
schools with principals who reported a very high emphasis on academic success in their school.
	
z Year 4 and Year 8 students with a high sense of school belonging had higher achievement in 
mathematics and science than students with little sense of school belonging. This relationship was 
stronger at Year 8 than at Year 4.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, higher percentages of students at more affluent schools than those from 
more disadvantaged schools reported a high sense of school belonging.
	
z Students who attended schools whose principals reported hardly any problems with school discipline 
had higher achievement in mathematics and science than students from schools whose principals 
reported moderate to severe problems with school discipline.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students at more affluent schools than more disadvantaged schools 
with principals who reported hardly any problems with school discipline.
	
z Students with mathematics and science teachers who reported that their schools were very safe and 
orderly had higher achievement in mathematics and science than students whose teachers reported 
that their schools were less than safe and orderly.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more affluent schools than from more disadvantaged 
schools with mathematics and science teachers who reported that their schools were very safe and 
orderly.
	
z Students who never or almost never experienced bullying (48% of both Year 4 and Year 8 students) 
had higher achievement in mathematics and science than students who reported being bullied about 
monthly or about weekly.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more affluent schools than from more disadvantaged 
schools who reported never being bullied.
	
z 7% of Year 4 students and 8% of Year 8 students reported being absent from school at least once a 
week. Students with this level of absenteeism had significantly lower achievement in mathematics and 
science than students who reported never or almost never being absent (around 50% of students).
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more disadvantaged schools than from more affluent 
schools who were absent once a week.
	
z 48% of Year 4 students and 61% of Year 8 students reported coming to school feeling tired every day or 
almost every day. There was a higher percentage of Year 4 students from more disadvantaged schools 
than from more affluent schools who reported coming to school feeling tired every day or almost 
every day.
	
z 37% of Year 4 students and 34% of Year 8 students reported coming to school feeling hungry every 
day or almost every day. There was a higher percentage of Year 4 students from more disadvantaged 
schools than from more affluent schools who reported coming to school feeling hungry every day or 
almost every day. There was no significant difference at Year 8.

Executive summary
xvii
Mathematics teacher preparation, professional development and 
job satisfaction (Chapter 4)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 4 for detailed analysis.
	
z While almost all students were taught mathematics by teachers with at least a bachelor’s degree or 
equivalent, only 21% of Year 4 students and 34% of Year 8 students were taught by teachers with a 
postgraduate university degree, compared to 33% of Year 4 students and 39% of Year 8 students, on 
average, internationally.
	
z At Year 4, 83% of students were taught mathematics by teachers who majored in primary education, and 
a further 12% were taught mathematics by teachers who majored in mathematics as well as primary 
education.
	
z At Year 8, 76% of students were taught mathematics by teachers who majored in either or both 
mathematics and mathematics education, with the remaining 24% being taught by mathematics 
teachers who had not majored in either mathematics or mathematics education, and who could be 
considered to be teaching out-of-field.
	
z Mathematics teachers were asked about the professional development they had taken in the previous 
two years. At both Year 4 and Year 8, the most common topic was mathematics curriculum.
	
z Mathematics teachers were asked about their need for future professional development opportunities in 
different topic areas. At both Year 4 and Year 8, the most popular topic was improving students’ critical 
thinking or problem-solving skills.
	
z 44% of Year 4 students and 47% of Year 8 students were taught by mathematics teachers who reported 
that they were very satisfied with their careers. There were no significant differences in mathematics 
achievement scores according to the level of teacher job satisfaction.

xviii
Executive summary
Teaching mathematics (Chapter 5)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 5 for detailed analysis.
	
z 86% of Year 4 students and 82% of Year 8 students had been taught all of the mathematics topics 
before or during the relevant year level, compared to the international average of 78% (at both year 
levels). At Year 4, all sub-domains had a coverage of over 80%. At Year 8, all sub-domains except for 
algebra had a coverage of about 80% of students; algebra was lower at 58% of students. Internationally, 
69% of students had been taught the algebra topics before or during Year 8.
	
z 68% of Year 4 students and 39% of Year 8 students reported high clarity of instruction in their 
mathematics lessons. These students had higher achievement in mathematics than those who reported 
low clarity of instruction.
	
z Approximately 25% of Year 4 and Year 8 students reported disorderly behaviour in most mathematics 
lessons. Students who reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons had higher mathematics 
achievement than students who reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons.
	
z While there were no significant differences between socioeconomic groups at Year 8, a higher 
percentage of Year 4 students who attended more disadvantaged schools than more affluent schools 
reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons.
	
z Approximately 17% of Year 4 and Year 8 students were taught mathematics by teachers who reported 
that their teaching was limited very little by students not ready for instruction, with around 71-75% 
taught by mathematics teachers who reported that their teaching was limited some. Students whose 
mathematics teachers reported that their teaching was limited very little had higher achievement in 
mathematics than students whose mathematics teachers reported that their teaching was limited a lot.
	
z There was a higher percentage of Year 4 students who attended more affluent schools than more 
disadvantaged schools who had mathematics teachers reported that their teaching was limited very 
little by students not ready for instruction. There were no significant differences by socioeconomic 
composition of the school at Year 8.

Executive summary
xix
Science teacher preparation, professional development and  
job satisfaction (Chapter 6)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 6 for detailed analysis.
	
z While almost all Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students were taught science by teachers with at least a 
bachelor’s degree or equivalent, only 21% of Year 4 students and 38% of Year 8 students were taught by 
teachers with a postgraduate university degree, compared to the international averages of 34% and 41%, 
respectively.
	
z At Year 4, 83% of students were taught science by teachers who majored in primary education, with a 
further 9% taught science by teachers who majored in science as well as primary education.
	
z At Year 8, 94% of students were taught science by teachers who majored in either or both of science 
and science education, with the remaining 6% being taught by science teachers who had not majored in 
either science or science education, and who could be considered to be teaching out-of-field.
	
z Science teachers were asked about the professional development they had taken over the previous two 
years. At both Year 4 and Year 8, the most common topic was assessing individual students’ needs.
	
z Students’ science teachers were asked about their need for future professional development 
opportunities in different topic areas. At Year 4, the most popular topic was integrating technology into 
science instruction. At Year 8, it was improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills.
	
z 46% of Year 4 students and 36% of Year 8 students were taught by science teachers who reported that 
they were very satisfied with their careers. There were no significant differences in science achievement 
scores according to the level of job satisfaction reported by students’ teachers.

xx
Executive summary
Teaching science (Chapter 7)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 7 for detailed analysis.
	
z 68% of Year 4 students and 60% of Year 8 students had been taught all of the science topics before or 
during the relevant year level, compared to the international averages of 65% and 61% (respectively).
	
z 64% of Year 4 students and 38% of Year 8 students reported high clarity of instruction in their science 
lessons.
	
z At Year 4, there was no significant differences in science achievement according to the clarity of 
instruction in science lessons. However, at Year 8, students who reported high clarity of instruction 
in their science lessons had higher achievement in science than students who reported low clarity of 
instruction.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, over 70% of students had teachers who placed a lot of emphasis on 
encouraging students to ask questions about scientific phenomena and having students predict the 
outcomes of experiments of investigations, the two most popular scientific inquiry practices.
	
z 58% of Year 4 students and 84% of Year 8 students conducted experiments in their science lessons at 
least once a month.
	
z Approximately 20% of Year 4 students and Year 8 students reported disorderly behaviour in most 
science lessons. Students who reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons had higher science 
achievement than students who reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons.
	
z There was a higher percentage of Year 4 and Year 8 students who attended more disadvantaged 
schools than more affluent schools who reported disorderly behaviour in most science lessons.
	
z Around 18% of Year 4 and 15% of Year 8 students were taught science by teachers who reported that 
their teaching was limited very little by students not ready for instruction. Around 73% of students were 
taught by science teachers who reported that their teaching was limited some. Students whose science 
teachers reported that their teaching was limited very little had higher achievement than students 
whose science teachers reported that their teaching was limited a lot.

Executive summary
xxi
Student attitudes and aspirations (Chapter 8)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 8 for detailed analysis.
	
z In general, students who said they liked mathematics or science, or were confident in learning these 
subjects or valued them also scored higher on average in assessments than students who did not.
	
z 68% of Year 4 students and 42% of Year 8 students reported that they very much like or somewhat like 
learning mathematics. And 77% of Year 4 students and 62% of Year 8 students reported that they very 
much like or somewhat like learning science.
	
z 67% of Year 4 students and 45% of Year 8 students reported that they were very or somewhat confident 
in mathematics; 69% of Year 4 students and 49% of Year 8 students reported that they were very or 
somewhat confident in science.
	
z Year 8 students tended to value mathematics, with 74% strongly or somewhat valuing mathematics. 
However, levels of valuing science were lower, with 63% of Year 8 students strongly or somewhat 
valuing science.
	
z In general, a greater proportion of female students than male students did not like learning mathematics 
and science, were not confident learning these subjects, and did not value them more. This did not hold 
for Year 4 students’ like of learning science or confidence in science, where there was no significant 
difference between female and male students.
	
z In general, a greater proportion of students from affluent backgrounds (that is, had many books or 
educational resources in the home) very much liked learning mathematics and science, were very 
confident learning these subjects, and strongly valued them more than students from a disadvantaged 
background (that is, had few books or educational resources in the home), while the reverse was true 
for not liking learning mathematics and science, being not confident learning these subjects, and not 
valuing them.
	
z Female students held higher ambitions than male students, with a greater percentage aiming for 
university study. Students from affluent backgrounds were far more likely to aspire to university than 
those from disadvantaged backgrounds, with the majority of those from disadvantaged backgrounds 
expecting that the completion of secondary school would be the highest education level they 
would achieve.

xxii
Executive summary
Students’ use of technology (Chapter 9)
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 9 for detailed analysis.
	
z 46% of Year 4 students and 74% of Year 8 students had high digital self-efficacy; a further 45% of Year 4 
students and 24% of Year 8 students had medium digital self-efficacy.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, there was a strong relationship between digital self-efficacy and mathematics 
and science achievement. Students with high digital self-efficacy had higher mathematics and science 
achievement than those with medium digital self-efficacy, who had higher achievement than those with 
low digital self-efficacy.
	
z There were significant differences in digital self-efficacy between female and male students at Year 4 
only; there were more male students with high or medium digital self-efficacy than female students, and 
more female students with low digital self-efficacy than male students.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, a greater proportion of students from affluent backgrounds (that is, had 
many books or educational resources in the home) had high digital self-efficacy than students from a 
disadvantaged background (that is, had few books or educational resources in the home).
	
z Year 8 students most commonly used the internet to access assignments posted online by their teacher 
or access the textbook or other course materials. The least common activity was accessing learning 
games or activities related to mathematics or science.

xxiii
READER’S GUIDE
Sample surveys
TIMSS is conducted as a sample survey in most countries. In a sample survey, a sample of students is 
selected to represent the population of students at a particular year level in a given country. The samples 
are designed and conducted so that they provide reliable estimates about their representative population. 
A sample survey is more economical for a school to undertake and less of a burden than a full census of a 
population.
The sample design for TIMSS is known as ‘a two-stage stratified cluster sample design’. Stage 1 samples the 
schools and Stage 2 identifies a single mathematics classroom selected at random from the target year level 
in the chosen sampled schools.
Statistical weights are used to adjust for any differences arising from intended features of the design (for 
example, to over-sample minorities) or non-participation by students who were selected. In this way we can 
provide measures of achievement for the population, based on the responses of a sample. Please refer to 
the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier et al., 2024) for more information about sampling in TIMSS.
TIMSS achievement scales
TIMSS uses item response theory (IRT) methods to summarise the achievement of students on a scale 
with a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 100 (please refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von 
Davier et al., 2024) for more information about IRT methods). It should be noted that the results for Year 4 
and Year 8 should not be compared, nor should the results for mathematics and science at a particular year 
level. While the scales are expressed in the same numerical units, they are not directly comparable such 
that conclusions could be drawn about how much learning in mathematics equals how much learning in 
science (or how much learning at Year 4 equals how much learning at Year 8). That is, achievement on the 
TIMSS scales cannot be described in absolute terms (like all such scales developed using IRT technology). 
Comparisons can be made only in terms of relative performance (higher or lower), for example, among 
countries and population groups as well as over time.
The TIMSS mathematics and science scales for Year 4 and Year 8 were based on those established in 
the first cycle of the study (TIMSS 1995) and the methodology enables comparable trend measures from 
assessment to assessment within each year level (refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier et al., 
2024) for more information).

xxiv
Reader’s guide
TIMSS context questionnaire scales
Many of the TIMSS 2023 context questionnaire items were developed to be combined into scales that 
measure a single underlying construct or characteristic (e.g. school emphasis on academic success; 
confidence in learning mathematics).
For reporting, the scales were constructed using IRT scaling methods. Data from selected context 
questionnaire responses were placed on a scale constructed with a mean of 10 and a standard deviation of 
2. Please refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (Von Davier et al., 2024) for more information about the 
creation of these scales.
Students were then able to be classified into groups that corresponded to high, middle, and low values on 
each construct. To enable interpretation of the groups, the cut-points on the scale defining the groups were 
described in terms of combinations of response categories (e.g. Year 4 students classified as ‘very much 
liking mathematics’ are those with a scale score greater than or equal to a cut-point defined for the scale, 
10.6 in this case, corresponding to ‘agree a lot’ with 4 of 7 statements and ‘agree a little’ with the other 3 
statements, on average). The definitions of the groups are included for each context scale at the start of the 
section that describes the findings for that scale, while the cut-points defining the categories are provided in 
Appendix A.
Standard errors and confidence intervals
In this and other reports, student achievement is often described by a mean or average score. For TIMSS, 
each mean score is calculated from the sample of students who undertook the assessments. These sample 
means approximate the actual mean score (known as the population mean) that would have been derived 
had all students in Australia participated in the TIMSS assessment.
If another sample of students was chosen on a different day, it is highly likely that the sample mean would be 
slightly different. Indeed, the sample mean is just one point along the range of student achievement scores, 
so more information is needed to gauge whether the sample mean is an underestimation or overestimation 
of the population mean.
In this report, means are presented with an associated standard error. The standard error is an estimate of 
the error in the estimate of the population mean from the sample (refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report 
(Von Davier et al., 2024) for more information about the estimation of standard errors in TIMSS). The size 
of the sample, as well as the variance or spread of the scores within the sample, can affect the size of the 
standard error. Smaller samples, or samples with a greater spread in scores, will have larger standard errors.
The calculation of confidence intervals can assist our assessment of a sample mean’s precision as an 
estimate of the population mean. Confidence intervals provide a range of scores within which we are 
‘confident’ that the population mean actually lies. The confidence interval is within plus or minus 1.96 
standard errors of the sample mean. A larger standard error results in a larger confidence interval, and 
a greater likelihood that the confidence intervals of two means will overlap and, therefore, reduce any 
difference to non-significance (see the next section on statistical significance).
Statistical significance
The term ‘significantly’ is used throughout the report to describe a difference that meets the requirements 
of statistical significance at the 0.05 level, indicating that the difference is real, and would be found in at 
least 95 analyses out of 100 if the comparison were to be repeated. It is not to be confused with the term 
‘substantial’, which is qualitative and based on judgement rather than statistical comparisons. A difference 
may appear substantial but not be statistically significant (due to factors that affect the size of the standard 
errors around the estimate, for example) while another difference may seem small but reach statistical 
significance because the estimate was more accurate.

Reader’s guide
xxv
Rounding of figures
Due to rounding to eliminate decimals, some percentages in tables and figures may not exactly add to 
the totals. Totals, differences and averages are always calculated on the basis of exact numbers and are 
rounded only after calculation. When standard errors have been rounded to one decimal place and the value 
0.0 is shown, this does not imply that the standard error is zero, but that it is smaller than 0.05.
Correlational analysis
An analysis of the correlation between two variables can be used to investigate the association between 
them. If there is a significant positive correlation, it does not imply that one factor depends on the other or 
that there is a cause-effect relationship between them – it simply means that they occur together. Further 
analysis and investigation are needed to determine the nature of the association.
The most commonly used measure is the Pearson correlation coefficient, which is abbreviated as r. The 
correlation coefficient measures the strength between two variables. Values of the correlation coefficient 
can range from –1 (a negative correlation – as one value increases the other value decreases) to a +1 
(a positive correlation – as one value increases the other value increases).
Pearson correlation coefficients between all the main scales reported on in this volume and mathematics 
and science achievement are available in Appendix B.
In this report, as a general rule, correlation coefficients between 0.10 and 0.29 represent a small association, 
correlation coefficients between 0.30 and 0.49 represent a moderate association, and correlation coefficients 
of 0.50 and above represent a strong association.
Students as the unit of analysis
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, the results from the school and teacher questionnaires are 
presented with regard to students. That is, each result is reported as the percentage of students who 
attended schools that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students taught by teachers 
who responded in a particular way. This is due to the fact that the sample for TIMSS is designed to be 
representative of the student population, not the teacher or school population.
When reporting results from the teacher questionnaire, results are reported separately for mathematics and 
science teachers. At Year 4, the results were very similar, as most Year 4 students have the same classroom 
teacher for both mathematics and science.
International comparisons
TIMSS compares results to ‘all countries, on average’ or to the international average or to individual countries 
– most often other English-speaking countries and high-performing countries (such as Singapore), or the 
countries that had notable results for that variable.
The international average refers to the mean score or percentage of all countries for which we have data on 
that variable from those who participated in TIMSS 2023 at that year level.
Notes about participating countries
Some countries have long official names. To facilitate the reading of the TIMSS reports, these countries are 
referred to by their shortened form in the text (for example, Hong Kong, Iran, Korea and Macao), rather than 
their official names (for example, Hong Kong SAR; Iran, Islamic Republic of; Korea, Republic of; Macao SAR).

xxvi
Reader’s guide
Some countries choose to administer TIMSS to grades other than the fourth or eighth years of formal 
schooling. Norway chose to assess the fifth and ninth grades to obtain better comparisons with Denmark, 
Finland and Sweden. South Africa assessed the fifth and ninth grades to better suit their curricula and to 
maintain trend measurement. Türkiye also chose to assess students in the fifth grade.
While TIMSS 2023 has transitioned fully to an online assessment, 8 countries conducted TIMSS 2023 as a 
paper-based assessment, but which only included 2019 trend items (Bulgaria, Cote d’lvoire, Cyprus, Iran, Iraq, 
Kuwait, Morocco and South Africa).
New Zealand and Cote d’lvoire did not satisfy the guidelines for minimum school participation rates at the 
Year 8 level, and therefore achievement data could not be reliably estimated. New Zealand and Cote d’lvoire 
results have been reported ‘below the line’ in the international results in mathematics and science at Year 8 
and have not been included in calculating the TIMSS international average or reported in trends. Results for 
New Zealand and Cote d’lvoire at Year 8 have not been included in this report. 
Iraq administered the Year 4 TIMSS 2023 assessment but is not included in the TIMSS 2023 international 
results (and therefore is not included in this report).
Definitions of background characteristics
There are various definitions used in this report that are particular to the Australian context, as well as many 
that are used internationally. This section provides an explanation for those that are not self-evident.
Gender
For reporting purposes, the gender of the student is taken from information provided by the school. In TIMSS 
2023, in Australia, the options were female, male or ‘other’. In TIMSS 2023, less than 1% of Australian Year 
4 and Year 8 students were identified as ‘other’, meaning that reporting of results for these students is not 
possible.
Number of books in the home
This variable is used as a proxy for socioeconomic status, where information about parents’ occupations, 
education and wealth are not available. It is derived from student self-reports of the number of books in their 
homes. Their responses have been grouped so that:
	
z a few books equals 25 or fewer books
	
z an average number of books equals between 26 and 200 books
	
z many books equals more than 200 books. 
While the relationship between the number of books in the home and student achievement is not definitive, 
there is a very strong relationship between the two.
Number of books in the home is used for student-level socioeconomic background for Year 4 students.
Educational resources in the home
The presence or absence of educational resources in the home expresses potential advantage or 
disadvantage for students that may reflect the ability of parents to provide materially for their children or 
indicate differences in practical and psychological support for academic achievement. These resources may 
be physical, such as books or an internet connection, or take the form of more intangible attributes such as 
parental education or occupation.
The Home educational resources scale was created, using Year 8 students’ responses to 3 items:
	
z parents’ educational background
	
z number of books in the home
	
z home study supports – students having their own room and an internet connection at home.

Reader’s guide
xxvii
Students with many resources had a score on the scale of at least 11.4, which corresponded to their 
reporting that they had more than 25 books in the home along with both home study supports (own room 
and an internet connection), and that at least one of their parents had finished university, on average. In 
contrast, students with few resources had a scale score no higher than 9.1, which corresponded to their 
reporting that they had 10 or fewer books in the home, that they had both home study supports, and that 
neither of their parents had proceeded beyond upper secondary school, on average. All other students were 
classified as having some resources.
The cut-points for the Home education resources index have been adjusted for TIMSS 2023. In TIMSS 
2019, students with many resources had a score on the scale that corresponded to their reporting that they 
had more than 100 books in the home along with both home study supports (own room and an internet 
connection), and that at least one of their parents had finished university, on average. Students with few 
resources had a scale score that corresponded to their reporting that they had 25 or fewer books in the 
home, that they had neither their own room nor an internet connection, and that neither of their parents had 
proceeded beyond upper secondary school, on average. Therefore, the index has shifted along the scale 
since TIMSS 2019.
Please note that, as Australia did not participate in an early learning survey, completed by parents, 
information about parental education, and therefore the Home Resources for Learning scale, is not available 
for Year 4 students.
The Home educational resources scale is used for student-level socioeconomic background for Year 8 
students.
Geographic location of the school
In Australia, in TIMSS 2023, the participating schools were coded using the Australian Bureau of Statistics’ 
Australian statistical geography standard (ASGS) remoteness structure, (ABS, 2011), which has superseded 
the MCEETYA classification of geographic location.
The following categories are used to report geographic location using the ASGS remoteness structure: 
	
z major cities, which includes all major cities of Australia
	
z regional areas, which includes all inner regional and outer regional areas in Australia
	
z remote areas, which includes all remote and very remote areas in Australia.
In this report, regional areas and remote areas have been combined into one category due to the large 
standard errors attached to schools in remote areas.
Teacher education
One path to becoming a qualified teacher in Australia is to complete a graduate diploma in education, after 
completion of an undergraduate degree. For the purposes of this report, given that the graduate diploma is 
necessary for teacher accreditation, the graduate diploma has been included in the same category as the 
bachelor’s degree or equivalent. Please note that this was not the case in TIMSS 2011 (when the graduate 
diploma was included as a postgraduate degree). Accordingly, responses to the teacher-education variable in 
cycles from TIMSS 2015 onwards cannot be compared with cycles from TIMSS 2011 and earlier.


1
CHAPTER 1
INTRODUCTION
1.1	
What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is an international study that 
assesses the mathematical and scientific knowledge and understanding of Year 4 and Year 8 students. 
TIMSS is directed by the International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA), an 
independent international cooperative of national research institutions and government agencies that has 
been conducting studies of cross-national achievement in a wide range of subjects since 1959. 
Australia has participated in TIMSS since the first cycle in 1995 and in every 4-year cycle since then and over 
this 28-year period has collected rich data about trends in mathematics and science achievement. The 2023 
cycle of TIMSS completed its transition to a completely online assessment. 
To inform educational policy in the participating countries, TIMSS also collects extensive background 
information that addresses concerns about the quantity, quality and content of instruction. This background 
information is collected through a series of questionnaires for students, parents, teachers, principals and 
curriculum specialists. 
Australia was one of 64 countries, and 6 regions or benchmarking participants,1 that participated in TIMSS 
2023 together with around 5,500 Year 4 students and 8,500 Year 8 students. These students completed the 
tests in mathematics and science and answered questionnaires on their background and experiences in 
learning these subjects at school. School principals and the students’ mathematics and science teachers 
also completed detailed questionnaires. 
For comparability across countries and across assessments, data collection is conducted at the end of 
the school year – typically, March to June in northern-hemisphere countries and August to November in 
southern-hemisphere countries. 
TIMSS 2023 was managed by the TIMSS & PIRLS International Study Center at Boston College in the United 
States. Sampling procedures were overseen by RTI International and the Sampling Unit at IEA Hamburg. The 
IEA Secretariat and the TIMSS & PIRLS International Study Center oversaw the translation and verification 
processes as well as the quality-assurance program; and IEA Hamburg was responsible for overseeing the 
data collection, data processing and data analysis. 
Appendix A of Volume I provides more information about the operations and procedures involved in 
TIMSS 2023.
1	
A benchmarking participant is a province or region that participated in TIMSS for its own internal benchmarking. Data from these provinces are not included in the 
international averages or medians and are not included in this national report.

2
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
1.2	
What are the main goals of TIMSS?
The main goal of TIMSS is to assist countries to monitor and evaluate their mathematics and science 
teaching across time and across year levels. TIMSS offers countries an opportunity to: 
	
z collect comprehensive and internationally comparable data about the mathematics and science concepts, 
processes and attitudes that students have learnt by Year 4 and Year 8 
	
z assess progress internationally in mathematics and science learning across time for students in Year 4 
and Year 8
	
z understand the contexts in which students learn best since TIMSS enables international comparisons of 
the key policy variables in relation to school curricula, modes of instruction and provision of resources 
that result in higher levels of student achievement
	
z use TIMSS to address internal policy issues – for example, to examine the performance of population 
subgroups and address equity concerns.
1.3	
Research model for IEA studies
TIMSS is based on a research model that uses the curriculum, in broad terms, as its foundation. The TIMSS 
curriculum model includes 3 curriculum levels, considered in relation to the context in which they occur. 
These levels are shown in Figure 1.1.
National, social
and educational
context
School, teacher
and classroom
context
Student
outcomes and
characteristics
Intended
curriculum
Implemented
curriculum
Attained
curriculum
FIGURE 1.1 The TIMSS curriculum model

Chapter 1 Introduction
3
The research questions associated with each of the curriculum levels are: 
	
z the intended curriculum – defined as the curriculum as specified at national or system level. What are 
mathematics and science students around the world expected to learn? How do countries vary in their 
intended goals, and what characteristics of education systems, schools and students influence the 
development of these goals? How should the education system be organised to facilitate this learning? 
	
z the implemented curriculum – defined as the curriculum as interpreted and delivered by classroom 
teachers. What is actually taught in classrooms? Who teaches it? What opportunities are provided for 
students to learn mathematics and science? How do instructional practices vary among countries and 
what factors influence these variations? 
	
z the attained curriculum – which is the part of the curriculum that is learnt by students, as demonstrated 
by their attitudes and achievements. What mathematics and science concepts, processes and attitudes 
have students learnt? What factors are linked to students’ opportunity to learn, and how do these factors 
influence students’ achievements?
The data describing the intended curriculum were gathered through the curriculum questionnaire, which 
asked about the mathematics and science curricula, school organisational approaches and instructional 
practices. This extensive questionnaire was developed in Australia by the ACER, reviewed by curriculum 
experts in each state and territory education department, and then submitted to the International Study 
Center. Further information about the curriculum and education policies is available through the TIMSS 2023 
encyclopedia (Reynolds et al., 2024).
1.4	
Components of TIMSS 2023
In Australia, students who participated in TIMSS 2023 completed an online assessment that contained 
an even distribution of mathematics and science items. The online assessment was designed to be 
administered in 2 sessions, separated by a short break. Year 4 sessions were 36 minutes and Year 8 
sessions were 45 minutes. Each student also completed a student questionnaire.
The contextual questionnaires
Central to the TIMSS research model is the idea that it is important to understand the contexts in which 
students learn, as well as to assess achievement. After the achievement data were collected from students, 
each student completed a student questionnaire. Teacher and school questionnaires were also administered 
to the mathematics and science teacher(s) of each selected class and to the principal of the school.
The student questionnaire sought information on home contexts, and on students’ characteristics and 
attitudes towards learning mathematics and science.
The teacher questionnaire asked about teacher preparation and experience, pedagogical practices, use of 
technology, assessment, assignment of homework, school and classroom climate, and whether the TIMSS 
topics had been covered in class.
The school questionnaire, answered by the principal (or the principal’s designate), sought descriptive 
information about school characteristics, instructional time, resources and technology, school climate for 
learning, students’ school readiness, and principal preparation and experience.

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics

benchmarking entities) participated in TIMSS 2023. In total, over 650,000 students participated worldwide. 
Eight countries completed a paper-based assessment that comprised TIMSS 2019 trend items only. The 
participating countries are shown in Table 1.1.

Within Australia
The international sample design for TIMSS is generally referred to as ‘a two-stage stratified cluster sample 
design’. Stage 1 samples schools, which in Australia are stratified by jurisdiction,2 sector, geographic location 
and a socioeconomic variable. The intention is that the sample drawn will be representative of each of these 
strata. Stage 2 samples one or two intact classrooms from the target year in sampled schools. 
In most countries, 150 schools and one classroom in each school were selected to participate (resulting 
in at least 4,000 participating students per country). In some countries, including Australia, a larger sample 
of schools and students participated to allow for meaningful comparisons to be made between different 
sections of the school population. This enabled the production of reliable estimates for each of the 
Australian jurisdictions. 
In Australia, 283 primary schools and 276 secondary schools participated in the TIMSS 2023 data collection. 
2	
In this report, the Australian states and territories are referred to collectively as the ‘ jurisdictions’.

Chapter 1 Introduction
5
At least one intact class from the relevant year level from each school was selected, resulting in a sample of 
5,470 Year 4 students and 8,442 Year 8 students. For more information about sampling and the Australian 
TIMSS sample, please refer to Appendix A of Volume I. 
Statistical weighting enables the sampled students to represent the total student population at each year 
level (for more information about weighting, please refer to the Reader’s guide). The weighted numbers for 
Australia for Year 4 and Year 8, along with the numbers of participating schools and students, are shown in

TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
1.6	
TIMSS and Australia’s National Assessment Program
TIMSS is a key part of the National Assessment Program (NAP). Components of NAP include the National 
Assessment Program – Literacy and Numeracy (NAPLAN), which is conducted annually for every 
student in Years 3, 5, 7 and 9; the national sample assessments of civics and citizenship, information and 
communication technology (ICT) literacy, and science literacy; and the international assessments, which 
comprise – in addition to TIMSS – the IEA’s Progress in International Reading Literacy Study (PIRLS) and 
the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development’s Programme for International Student 
Assessment (PISA).
Results from these NAP assessments allow for nationally comparable reporting of progress towards the 
Alice Springs (Mparntwe) education declaration (Education Council, 2019). This declaration sets out a vision 
for education in Australia that promotes excellence and equity to enable all Australians to become confident 
and creative individuals, successful learners, and active and informed community members.
The Australian Curriculum, Assessment and Reporting Authority (ACARA) reports on the NAP assessments 
annually in its National report on schooling in Australia, which is the main vehicle for reporting against 
nationally agreed key performance measures defined in the Measurement framework for schooling in 
Australia 2020 (ACARA, 2023). 
1.7	
Structure of this report
This is the second of two reports that look at the results of TIMSS 2023 and Australia’s performance. 
Volume I focuses specifically on the achievement results, detailing Australia’s results within the international 
context, and presents the results for the Australian jurisdictions and for different demographic groups within 
Australia, including female and male students. It also examines TIMSS 2023 results in the content and 
cognitive domains.
This report, Volume II, presents the results from the contextual questionnaires and examines the home, 
school, and classroom contexts in which learning and achievement occur, as well as student attitudes.
Each chapter focuses on different indicators that cover the school community, the school learning 
environment, mathematics and science teacher characteristics, mathematics and science classroom 
learning environments, and students’ attitudes and beliefs and use of technology. Together, the different 
indicators of student and school life illustrate some of the many key aspects that make up the school 
experience.

7
CHAPTER 2
COMPOSITION OF SCHOOLS AND 
SCHOOL RESOURCES
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 2 for detailed analysis.
	
z Year 4 and Year 8 students who attended more affluent schools had higher achievement in mathematics 
and science than students at more disadvantaged schools.
	
z Higher percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students from schools in major cities than from schools in 
regional and remote areas were at schools with more affluent student bodies.
	
z At Year 4, 42% of Australian students attended schools where more than 90% of students had English as 
their first language, compared to the international average of 66%. At Year 8, this was 44% of Australian 
students, compared to the international average of 63%.
	
z At Year 4, 48% of students attended schools where less than 25% of students begin school with literacy 
and numeracy skills. Those students recorded average mathematics and science scores that were 
around 30 points lower than those recorded by students at schools where 25–75% of the student 
population had literacy and numeracy skills when they began school.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more disadvantaged schools than from more affluent 
schools where less than 25% of students enter school with literacy and numeracy skills.
	
z 48% of Year 4 students and 35% of Year 8 students attended schools where mathematics instruction 
was somewhat affected by resource shortages, compared to the international averages of 62% 
and 60%.
	
z While there was no significant difference at Year 4, Year 8 students who attended schools where 
mathematics instruction was not affected by resource shortages achieved at a significantly higher level 
in mathematics than students who attended schools where instruction was somewhat affected.
	
z 60% of Year 4 students and 33% of Year 8 students attended schools where science instruction was 
somewhat affected by resource shortages, compared to the international averages of 63% and 60%.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, students who attended schools where science instruction was not affected 
by resource shortages achieved at a significantly higher level in science than students at schools where 
instruction was somewhat affected.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more disadvantaged schools than from more affluent 
schools where instruction was somewhat affected by resource shortages.

8
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
KEY FINDINGS
	
z At Year 4, 41% of Australian students attended schools with principals who had a postgraduate degree 
in educational leadership, the same as the international average. At Year 8, 51% of Australian students 
attended schools with a principal who had a postgraduate degree in educational leadership, compared 
to the international average of 45%.
	
z There was a higher percentage of Year 8 students from more affluent schools than from more 
disadvantaged schools with principals who had a postgraduate degree in educational leadership but 
this difference was not seen for Year 4 students.
2.1	
Introduction
The environment in which students learn can help or hinder their achievement. At a basic level, a lack of 
resources, such as adequate facilities, appropriately qualified staff and sufficient instructional resources, 
is going to make teaching and learning more difficult (Hanushek & Wößmann, 2017). Schools with greater 
proportions of students from groups that are considered educationally disadvantaged will also stretch 
resources and make teaching and learning more difficult (Martin et al., 2013).
This chapter draws on data collected in the TIMSS school questionnaire and focuses on the student body 
composition (such as socioeconomic and language background) and school resources, including principals’ 
qualifications.  
Chapter 3 focuses on the social and emotional components of the school environment. Together, Chapters 2 
and 3 of this report cover the characteristics of schools and school environments that can affect 
achievement.
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, results from the teacher questionnaire and the school questionnaire 
are presented with regard to students. This means that each result is either reported as the percentage of 
students who attended schools that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were 
taught by teachers or principals who responded in a particular way. Please see the Reader’s guide for more 
information.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 2 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors.
2.2	
School socioeconomic composition
The socioeconomic composition of schools influences student achievement (Liu et al., 2015). Accordingly, 
the TIMSS 2023 school questionnaire asked principals to report on the socioeconomic composition of 
their schools by indicating the percentages of students who came from economically affluent homes 
and from economically disadvantaged homes. The responses were used to create 3 categories of school 
socioeconomic composition:
	
z more affluent – schools where more than 25% of the student body comes from economically affluent 
homes and not more than 25% from economically disadvantaged homes
	
z more disadvantaged – schools where more than 25% of the student body comes from economically 
disadvantaged homes and not more than 25% from economically affluent homes
	
z neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged – all other response combinations.

Chapter 2 Composition of schools and school resources
9
This variable is used to investigate whether other variables of interest vary by the degree to which a school’s 
student body is relatively more affluent or more disadvantaged.
Figure 2.1 presents the proportions of students in each of the socioeconomic composition categories, along 
with their average mathematics and science scores.

Year 4 Australian students
	
z 43% attended more affluent schools, which was similar to the international average of 42%.

which was similar to the international average.

10
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
As can be seen in Figure 2.1, there is a clear relationship between the composition of the student body and 
student achievement for mathematics and science at both Year 4 and Year 8.  
At both Year 4 and Year 8, students who attended schools that were more affluent had higher average 
mathematics and science achievement than students who attended schools were neither more affluent nor 
more disadvantaged. Both groups had higher average achievement than students who attended schools that 
were more disadvantaged. All differences were statistically significant. 
In Australia, the gaps between Year 4 students who attended schools that were more affluent and those who 
attended schools that were more disadvantaged were about 76 points in mathematics achievement and 59 
points in science achievement. At Year 8, the gaps were about 68 points in mathematics achievement and 61 
points in science achievement.
Geolocation differences
In Australia, the geographic location of the school (schools in major cities, and regional and remote areas) 
relates to differences in achievement (see Volume I). These may be due to the characteristics of schools 
or classrooms. In this Volume II report, we also investigate whether certain variables of interest change 
depending on the geographic location of the school. Please see the Reader’s guide for more information 
about this variable.
Figure 2.2 presents the proportions of Australian students who attended schools of different socioeconomic 
composition according to school location. Australian schools’ socioeconomic composition differs according 
to the geographic location of the school.
At both Year 4 and Year 8, there was a significantly larger proportion of students at schools in major cities 
that had principals who reported their schools had more affluent student bodies than at schools in regional 
and remote areas.
In addition, at Year 8, there was a significantly larger proportion of students at schools in regional and remote 
areas that had principals who reported their schools had more disadvantaged student bodies than at schools 
in major cities.

2.3	
Language background of school populations 
When a student body is composed predominantly of students who do not have the language of instruction 
as a native language, educational and cultural complexities may challenge schools and teachers. The school 
questionnaire asked Australian principals what proportion of their student bodies had English as their first 
language. Their responses were grouped into 3 categories:
	
z more than 90% of students have the language of test as their native language
	
z 51–90% of students have the language of test as their native language
	
z 50% or fewer students have the language of test as their native language.
Figure 2.3 presents the proportions of students in each of 3 response categories, along with their average 
mathematics and science scores. The language of the test for Australian students was English. For the 
international averages, the language of test was the language of instruction for mathematics and science.
In Australia for Year 4 and Year 8 students, there were no significant differences in average achievement 
according to the language background of the student body.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 42% attended schools where more than 90% of students had the language of the test as their first 
language compared to the international average of 66%.
	
z 36% attended schools where 51–90% of students had the language of the test compared to the 
international average of 20%.
	
z 22% attended schools where 50% or less students had the language of the test compared to the 
international average of 15%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 44% attended schools where more than 90% of students had the language of the test compared to the 
international average of 63%.
	
z 40% attended schools where 51–90% of students had the language of the test compared to the 
international average of 18%.
	
z 15% attended schools where 50% or less students had the language of the test compared to the 
international average of 19%.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 2.4 presents the proportions of Australian students by the language background and socioeconomic 
composition of their schools. There are some differences in the language background of school populations 
according to school socioeconomic composition; these are statistically significant at Year 8 only.
The following differences in the language background of the school population according to socioeconomic 
composition of the school population were statistically significant at Year 8:
	
z there was a significantly larger proportion of students from more affluent schools in whose population

affluent nor more disadvantaged

from more affluent schools

nor more disadvantaged in whose population between 51–90% of students had English as their native 
language than from more disadvantaged schools 
	
z there was a significantly larger proportion of students from more disadvantaged schools in whose 
population less than 50% of students had English as their native language than from more affluent schools.

2.4	
Students begin school with literacy and numeracy skills
Year 4 principals were asked how many students in their school could do the following when they began their 
first year of primary school (more than 75%; 51–75%, 25–50%; less than 25%):
	
z recognise most of the letters of the alphabet
	
z read some words
	
z read sentences
	
z write letters of the alphabet
	
z write words other than their names
	
z count up to 100 or higher
	
z recognise written numbers from 1 to 10
	
z recognise written numbers higher than 10
	
z write numbers from 1 to 10
	
z do simple addition
	
z do simple subtraction.
Principals’ responses to these 11 items were combined to create the Schools where students begin the 
primary grades with literacy and numeracy skills scale. The 3 categories are comprised in the following way 
and all students were assigned to these based on their principals’ scale scores:
	
z on average, the more than 75% of students begin with skills category corresponds to principals who 
reported that more than 75% of students had 6 of the 11 skills and 51–75% had the other 5 skills
	
z on average the less than 25% begin with skills category corresponds to principals who reported that less 
than 25% of students had 6 of the 11 skills and 25–50% had the other 5 skills
	
z all other students attended schools where between 25–75% begin with skills.

14
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Figure 2.5 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
mathematics and science scores:
	
z 6% of Australian students attended schools where more than 75% begin with skills compared to the 
international average of 24%
	
z 47% attended schools where between 25–75% begin with skills compared to the international average 
of 56%
	
z 48% attended schools where less than 25% begin with skills compared to the international average of 20%.
As can be seen in there is a clear relationship between percentage of students who began school with skills 
and student achievement for mathematics and science at Year 4.  
Year 4 students who attended schools where more than 75% begin with skills had higher average 
mathematics and science achievement than students who attended schools where between 25–75% begin 
with skills. Both groups had higher average achievement than students who attended schools where less 
than 25% begin with skills. All differences were statistically significant. 
In Australia, students who attended schools where more than 75% begin with skills scored, on average, 61 
points more in mathematics and 46 points more in science than those who attended schools where less 
than 25% begin with skills.

2.5	
Instruction affected by mathematics resource shortages 
Principals were asked about the degree to which their school’s capacity to provide instruction was affected 
by a shortage of – or inadequacy in – general and mathematics instruction resources (not at all; a little; 
some; a lot).
General school resources:
	
z instructional materials (e.g. textbooks) 
	
z supplies (e.g. papers, pencils, materials)
	
z school buildings and grounds
	
z heating/cooling and lighting systems
	
z instructional space (e.g. classrooms)
	
z technologically competent staff
	
z audio-visual resources for delivery of instruction (e.g. interactive white boards, digital projectors)
	
z digital devices for student use.
Resources for mathematics instruction:
	
z teachers with a specialisation in mathematics
	
z computer software/applications for mathematics instruction
	
z library resources relevant to mathematics instruction
	
z calculators for mathematics instruction
	
z concrete objects or materials to help students understand quantities or procedures.
Principals’ responses to these 13 items were combined to create the Mathematics resource shortages scale. 
The 3 categories are comprised in the following way and all students were assigned to these based on their 
principals’ scale scores:
	
z on average, the not affected category  corresponds to principals who reported that shortages affected 
instruction not at all for 7 of the 13 resources and a little for the other 6 resources 
	
z on average, the affected a lot category corresponds to principals who reported that shortages affected 
instruction a lot for 7 of the 13 resources and some for the other 6 resources 
	
z all other Year 4 and Year 8 students attended schools where instruction was somewhat affected by 
resource shortages.
Figure 2.8 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
mathematics scores.

Students affected by mathematics resource shortages
FIGURE 2.8 The Mathematics resource shortages scale and student achievement in mathematics, Australia and the 
international averages 
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 48% attended schools where mathematics instruction was not affected by resource shortages compared 
to the international average of 32%.
	
z 48% attended schools where it was somewhat affected compared to the international average of 62%.
	
z 3% attended schools where mathematics instruction was affected a lot by resource shortages compared 
to the international average of 6%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 60% attended schools where mathematics instruction was not affected by resource shortages compared 
to the international average of 34%.
	
z 35% attended schools where it was somewhat affected compared to the international average of 60%.
	
z 5% attended schools where mathematics instruction was affected a lot by resource shortages compared 
to the international average of 6%.
Interestingly, as seen in Figure 2.8, resource shortages had a stronger relationship with mathematics 
scores for Australian Year 8 students than for Year 4 students. That is, there were no significant differences 
between the 3 groups in terms of average mathematics achievement at Year 4. However, Year 8 students 
who attended schools where mathematics instruction was not affected by resource shortages achieved an 
average mathematics score that was significantly higher than those students who attended schools where 
instruction was somewhat affected (a difference of 27 points).

18
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 2.9 presents the proportions of Australian students in each of the instruction affected by resource 
shortages categories by school socioeconomic composition. It shows that, in Australia, the percentages 
of students who attended schools affected by mathematics resource shortages varied according to the 
schools’ socioeconomic composition.
At Year 4, most of the variation was not significant. However, there was a significantly smaller proportion of 
students who attended more affluent schools where mathematics instruction was somewhat affected by 
resource shortages, compared to the proportions of students who attended more disadvantaged schools 
and schools that were neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged. These differences were statistically 
significant.
At Year 8, there were larger proportions of students who attended more affluent schools  and schools 
that were neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged where mathematics instruction was not affected 
by resource shortages compared to the proportion of students who attended schools that were more 
disadvantaged. 
Conversely, there was a larger proportion of students who attended more disadvantaged schools where 
mathematics instruction was somewhat affected by resource shortages, compared to the proportions of 
students who attended schools that were neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged and students who 
attended more affluent schools . These differences were statistically significant.

2.6	
Instruction affected by science resource shortages 
Principals were asked about the degree to which their school’s capacity to provide instruction was affected 
by a shortage of – or inadequacy in – general and science instruction (not at all; a little; some; a lot).
General school resources:
	
z instructional materials (e.g. textbooks)  
	
z supplies (e.g. papers, pencils, materials) 
	
z school buildings and grounds
	
z heating/cooling and lighting systems 
	
z instructional space (e.g. classrooms) 
	
z technologically competent staff
	
z audio-visual resources for delivery of instruction (e.g. interactive white boards, digital projectors)
	
z digital devices for student use.
Resources for science instruction:
	
z teachers with a specialisation in science
	
z computer software/applications for science instruction
	
z library resources relevant to science instruction   
	
z science equipment and materials for experiments.
Principals’ responses to the 12 items were combined to create the Science resource shortages scale. The 3 
categories of the scale are comprised in the following way and all students were assigned to these based on 
their principals’ scale scores:
	
z on average, the not affected category corresponds to principals who reported that not affected at all for 6 
of the 12 resources and a little for the other 6 resources 
	
z on average, the affected a lot category corresponds to principals who reported that shortages affected 
instruction a lot for 6 of the 12 resources and some for the other 6 resources
	
z all other Year 4 and Year 8 students attended schools where instruction was somewhat affected by 
resource shortages.
Figure 2.10 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
science scores.

Students affected by science resource shortages

20
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 36% attended schools where science instruction was not affected by resource shortages compared to the 
international average of 29%.
	
z 60% attended schools where it was somewhat affected compared to the international average of 63%.
	
z 4% attended schools where science instruction was affected a lot by resource shortages compared to the 
international average of 7%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 62% attended schools where science instruction was not affected by resource shortages compared to the 
international average of 33%.
	
z 33% attended schools where it was somewhat affected compared to the international average of 60%.
	
z 5% attended schools where science instruction was affected a lot by resource shortages compared to the 
international average of 6%.
As can be seen in Figure 2.10, there is a slight relationship between science resources shortages and student 
achievement in science. At both Year 4 and Year 8, Australian students who attended schools where science 
instruction was not affected by resource shortages achieved an average science score that was significantly 
higher than students who attended schools where instruction was somewhat affected. 
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 2.11 presents the proportions of Australian students in each of the instruction-affected-by- resource-
shortages categories, by school socioeconomic composition. It shows that, in Australia, the percentages 
of students who attended schools affected by science resource shortages varied according to the schools’ 
socioeconomic composition.
At Year 4, most of the variation was not significant. However, a smaller proportion of of students who 
attended more affluent schools were at schools where science instruction was somewhat affected by 
resource shortages, than the proportion of students who attended more disadvantaged schools. This 
difference was statistically significant.
At Year 8, a larger proportion of students at more affluent schools were at schools where science instruction 
was not affected by resource shortages compared to the proportion  of students at more disadvantaged 
schools. Conversely, there was a larger proportion of students at more disadvantaged schools where 
science instruction was somewhat affected by resource shortages, compared to the proportion of students 
at schools that were neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged, and the proportion at more affluent 
schools. These differences were statistically signif﻿ii cant.
