2.7	
Formal qualifications of principals 
TIMSS 2023 collected information on the formal qualifications of principals, including whether they had 
specialised training in educational leadership.
Table 2.1 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students by the formal education of their principals, 
including educational leadership qualifications. The table shows results for Australia and the international 
average.
At Year 4, 48% of Australian students attended schools whose principals had completed a postgraduate 
university degree (i.e. doctorate, master’s or other postgraduate degree), compared to the international 
average of 57%. In addition, 41% of Australian students attended schools whose principals had a 
postgraduate degree in educational leadership, a figure similar to the international average.
At Year 8, 61% of Australian students attended schools whose principals had completed a postgraduate 
university degree, compared to the international average of 59%. In addition, 51% of Australian students 
attended schools with principals who had a postgraduate degree in educational leadership, compared to the 
international average of 45%.
The percentages of students who attended schools with principals who had completed a postgraduate 
university degree varied widely among participating TIMSS countries. 
In the United States, 98% of students (at both year levels) attended schools whose principals had completed 
a postgraduate degree, compared to Japan, where only around 10% of students attended schools whose 
principals had completed a postgraduate degree. In many cases, the percentages of students who attended 
schools with principals who had completed postgraduate degrees was higher at Year 8 than at Year 4. This 
was true for Australia (48% at Year 4; 61% at Year 8), Ireland (67% at Year 4; 85% at Year 8) and England (30% 
at Year 4; 45% at Year 8).

Socioeconomic differences 
Table 2.2 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students by the formal education of their principals, 
including educational leadership qualifications, by school socioeconomic composition.
At Year 4, there were no significant differences between the socioeconomic groups in terms of principals’ 
formal education, except for a significantly larger proportion of students who attended more affluent schools 
had a principal with a postgraduate degree in educational leadership compared to students at neither more 
affluent nor more disadvantaged schools.
However, at Year 8, a significantly larger proportion of students who attended neither more affluent nor 
more disadvantaged schools had principals with a postgraduate university degree compared with students 
who attended more disadvantaged schools. In addition, a significantly larger proportion of students who 
attended more affluent schools or neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools had principals with 
a postgraduate degree in educational leadership compared with students who attended more disadvantaged 
schools.

2.8	
Principals’ experience  
Table 2.4 shows the percentages of students according to the experience of their principal – expressed as 
the number of years that principals had worked in that position. The table shows the results for Australia 
and the international average. There are no significant differences in the average number of years worked 
between principals in Australia and the international average.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 11% attended schools with principals who had 20 years or more experience as a principal, compared to 
the international average of 12%.
	
z 31% attended schools with principals who had at least 10 but less than 20 years’ experience, compared to 
the international average of 28%. 
	
z 29% attended schools with principals who had at least 5 but less than 10 years’ experience, compared to 
the international average of 27%. 
	
z 29% attended schools with principals who had less than 5 years of experience, compared to the 
international average of 33%. 
On average, Australian principals had 9 years of experience, similar to the international average (10 years).
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 9% attended schools with principals who had 20 years or more experience as a principal, compared to the 
international average of 11%.
	
z 28% attended schools with principals who had at least 10 but less than 20 years’ experience, the same as 
the international average.
	
z 29% attended schools with principals who had at least 5 but less than 10 years’ experience, compared to 
the international average of 27%.
	
z 34% attended schools with principals who had less than 5 years of experience, the same as the 
international average. 
On average, Australian principals had 9 years of experience, the same as the international average.

CHAPTER 3
SCHOOL CLIMATE, DISCIPLINE 
AND SAFETY
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 3 for detailed analysis.
	
z 10% of Year 4 students and 15% of Year 8 students attended schools whose principals reported a very 
high emphasis on academic success in their schools. These students had significantly higher average 
mathematics and science achievement than those from schools whose principals reported a high or 
medium emphasis on academic success.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more affluent schools than from more disadvantaged 
schools with principals who reported a very high emphasis on academic success in their school.
	
z Year 4 and Year 8 students with a high sense of school belonging had higher achievement in 
mathematics and science than students with little sense of school belonging. This relationship was 
stronger at Year 8 than at Year 4.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, higher percentages of students at more affluent schools than those from 
more disadvantaged schools reported a high sense of school belonging.
	
z Students who attended schools whose principals reported hardly any problems with school discipline 
had higher achievement in mathematics and science than students from schools whose principals 
reported moderate to severe problems with school discipline.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students at more affluent schools than more disadvantaged schools 
with principals who reported hardly any problems with school discipline.
	
z Students with mathematics and science teachers who reported that their schools were very safe and 
orderly had higher achievement in mathematics and science than students whose teachers reported 
that their schools were less than safe and orderly.
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more affluent schools than from more disadvantaged 
schools with mathematics and science teachers who reported that their schools were very safe and 
orderly.
	
z Students who never or almost never experienced bullying (48% of both Year 4 and Year 8 students) 
had higher achievement in mathematics and science than students who reported being bullied about 
monthly or about weekly.

Chapter 3 School climate, discipline and safety
27
KEY FINDINGS
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more affluent schools than from more disadvantaged 
schools who reported never being bullied.
	
z 7% of Year 4 students and 8% of Year 8 students reported being absent from school at least once a 
week. Students with this level of absenteeism had significantly lower achievement in mathematics and 
science than students who reported never or almost never being absent (around 50% of students).
	
z There was a higher percentage of students from more disadvantaged schools than from more affluent 
schools who were absent once a week.
	
z 48% of Year 4 students and 61% of Year 8 students reported coming to school feeling tired every day or 
almost every day. There was a higher percentage of Year 4 students from more disadvantaged schools 
than from more affluent schools who reported coming to school feeling tired every day or almost 
every day.
	
z 37% of Year 4 students and 34% of Year 8 students reported coming to school feeling hungry every 
day or almost every day. There was a higher percentage of Year 4 students from more disadvantaged 
schools than from more affluent schools who reported coming to school feeling hungry every day or 
almost every day. There was no significant difference at Year 8.
3.1	
Introduction
The social–emotional environment of a school, that is, the way the members of the school community 
experience the school, has an impact on teaching and learning (Martin et al., 2013). Factors such as the 
emphasis on academic success within the school community, feelings of safety in the school, and students’ 
sense of school belonging have all been found to influence student achievement (Hoy, 2012; Korpershoek et 
al., 2020; Kutsyuruba et al., 2015).
Chapter 3 focuses on the social and emotional components of the school environment that can affect 
achievement, using data collected through the school, student and teacher questionnaires. It completes the 
description of characteristics of schools and school environments begun in Chapter 2.
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, results from the teacher and school questionnaires are presented 
with regard to students. This means that each result is reported as the percentage of students who attended 
schools that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were taught by teachers or 
principals who responded in a particular way. Please see the Reader’s guide for more information.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 3 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors.

28
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
3.2	
Principals’ reports of school emphasis on academic success
Principals were asked about the academic climates of their schools, that is, the degree to which their school 
community supported and encouraged academic success (very high; high; medium; low; very low):
	
z teachers’ understanding of the school’s curricular goals
	
z teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum
	
z teachers’ expectations for student achievement
	
z teachers’ ability to inspire students
	
z parental involvement in school activities
	
z parental commitment to ensure that students are ready to learn
	
z parental expectations for student achievement
	
z parental support for student achievement
	
z students’ desire to do well in school
	
z students’ ability to reach school’s academic goals
	
z students’ respect for classmates who excel academically.
Principals’ responses to these 11 items were combined to create the School emphasis on academic success 
scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these based on 
their principals’ scale scores:
	
z on average, the very high emphasis on academic success category corresponds to principals who 
reported very high emphasis for 6 of the 11 aspects and the other 5 as high emphasis
	
z on average, the medium emphasis on academic success category corresponds to principals who reported 
6 of the 11 aspects as medium emphasis and the other 5 as high emphasis
	
z all other students attended schools with a high emphasis on academic success.
Figure 3.1 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
mathematics and science scores.

School emphasis on academic success
FIGURE 3.1 The School emphasis on academic success scale and student achievement in mathematics and science, 
Australia and the international averages
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 10% attended schools whose principals reported a very high emphasis on academic success compared to 
the international average of 9%.
	
z 63% attended schools with a high emphasis compared to the international average of 54%.
	
z 27% attended schools with a medium emphasis on academic success compared to the international 
average of 37%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 15% attended schools whose principals reported a very high emphasis on academic success compared to 
the international average of 10%.
	
z 40% attended schools with a high emphasis compared to the international average of 49%. 
	
z 45% attended schools with a medium emphasis on academic success compared to the international 
average of 41%.
Figure 3.1 shows there is a small relationship between the achievement of Australian students and 
principals’ reports of school emphasis on academic success: a higher school emphasis on academic 
success was associated with higher achievement (a correlation of around 0.22 at Year 4 and at Year 8). 

30
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
The difference between the average achievement of Australian students who attended schools whose 
principals reported a very high emphasis and those who reported a medium emphasis on academic success 
were gaps that were as follows:
	
z 81 points for Year 4 mathematics compared to the international average of 39 points
	
z 57 points for Year 4 science compared to the international average of 39 points
	
z 81 points for Year 8 mathematics compared to the international average of 61 points
	
z 67 points for Year 8 science compared to the international average of 58 points. 
These differences were statistically significant for both subjects at both Australian year levels. 
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 3.2 presents the percentages of Australian students in each of the 3 categories of the School 
emphasis on academic success scale according to school socioeconomic composition. It shows that in 
both Year 4 and Year 8, students who attended more affluent schools were more likely to have principals 
who reported a very high emphasis on academic success than students who attended more disadvantaged 
schools.
At Year 4, there was a significantly larger proportion of students who attended more affluent schools who 
had principals who reported a very high emphasis on academic success, compared to the proportion of 
students at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools. No students at more disadvantaged 
schools had principals who reported a very high emphasis on academic success. 
Also at Year 4, there was a significantly larger proportion of students who attended more affluent schools 
or neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools who had principals who reported a high emphasis 
on academic success, compared to the proportion of students at more disadvantaged schools. Conversely, 
there was a significantly larger proportion of Year 4 students who attended more disadvantaged schools 
who had principals who reported a medium emphasis on academic success, compared to the proportion of 
students at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools or more affluent schools.
At Year 8, there was a significantly larger proportion of students who attended more affluent schools and 
the proportion of students at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools who had principals who 
reported a high emphasis on academic success, compared to students at more disadvantaged schools. 
Conversely, there was a significantly larger proportion of Year 8 students who attended more disadvantaged 
schools who had principals who reported a medium emphasis on academic success  compared to the 
proportion of students at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools, and larger again than the 
proportion of students at more affluent schools.

3.3	
Students’ sense of belonging
Students were asked how much they agreed with the following statements about their feelings towards 
school (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; or disagree a lot):
	
z I like being in school.
	
z I feel safe when I am at school.
	
z I feel like I belong at this school.
	
z Teachers at this school care about me.
	
z I am proud to go to this school.
	
z Students in this school respect me. (Year 8 only)
	
z Students at this school like me the way I am.
Responses to these 7 items were combined to create the Students’ sense of school belonging scale. The 3 
categories are comprised in the following way:
	
z on average, Year 4 students with a high sense of school belonging corresponds to agree a lot with 4 of the 
6 statements and agree a little with the other 2 statements 
	
z on average, Year 4 students with little sense of school belonging corresponds to disagree a little with 4 of 
the 6 statements and agree a little with the other 2 statements
	
z all other Year 4 students had some sense of school belonging
	
z on average, Year 8 students with a high sense of school belonging corresponds to agree a lot with 4 of the 
7 statements and agree a little with the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, students with little sense of school belonging corresponds to disagree a little with 4 of the 7 
statements and agree a little with the other 3 statements
	
z all other Year 8 students had some sense of school belonging.

32
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Figure 3.3 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
mathematics and science scores.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 54% had a high sense of school belonging compared to the international average of 57%.
	
z 32% had some sense of school belonging compared to the international average of 30%.
	
z 14% had little sense of school belonging compared to the international average of 12%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 18% had a high sense of school belonging compared to the international average of 30%.
	
z 50% had some sense of school belonging compared to the international average of 49%.
	
z 32% had little sense of school belonging compared to the international average of 21%.
Figure 3.3 shows there is a small relationship between students’ achievement and their sense of school 
belonging; a higher sense of school belonging is associated with higher achievement. The relationship 
appears slightly stronger at Year 8 than at Year 4 (a correlation of 0.10 Year 4 and 0.23 at Year 8 with 
mathematics achievement and 0.04 at Year 4 and 0.20 at Year 8 with science achievement). 
Australian Year 4 students with a high sense of school belonging scored 22 points higher in mathematics 
achievement than those with little sense of school belonging and 14 points higher than those with some 
sense of school belonging (both differences were significant). There were no significant differences in 
science achievement at Year 4.
Australian Year 8 students with a high sense of school belonging scored 55 points higher in mathematics 
achievement and 46 points higher in science achievement than those with little sense of school belonging. 
All differences in achievement between the levels of sense of school belonging were significant.
Internationally, on average, the difference in mathematics or science achievement between high and low 
sense of belonging ranged between 17 and 25 score points.

Sense of school belonging
FIGURE 3.3 The Students’ sense of school belonging scale and student achievement in mathematics and science,

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 3.4 presents the percentages of Australian students in each of the 3 categories of the Students’ sense 
of school belonging scale, according to school socioeconomic composition.
At Year 4, a significantly larger proportion of students who attended more disadvantaged schools, as well as 
those who attended neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools reported little sense of school 
belonging compared to students who attended more affluent schools.  In addition, a larger proportion of 
students who attended more affluent schools compared to those who attended more disadvantaged schools 
reported a high sense of school belonging. 
At Year 8 a significantly larger proportion of students who attended more disadvantaged schools reported 
little sense of school belonging than students who attended more affluent schools and students who 
attended neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools. The difference between students who 
attended neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools and students who attended more affluent 
schools was also significant. In contrast, a larger proportion of students who attended more affluent schools, 
compared to those who attended more disadvantaged schools, reported a high sense of school belonging.

3.4	
Principals’ reports of school discipline problems
Principals were asked about the degree to which each of the following behaviours and issues was 
problematic among Year 4 or Year 8 students in their school (not a problem; minor problem; moderate 
problem; or severe problem):
	
z arriving late at school
	
z absenteeism (i.e. unjustified absences)
	
z classroom disturbance
	
z cheating
	
z swearing
	
z vandalism
	
z theft
	
z intimidation or verbal abuse among students (including texting, emailing etc.)
	
z physical injury to other students
	
z intimidation or verbal abuse of teachers or staff (including texting, emailing etc.)
	
z physical injury to teachers or staff (Year 8 only).
Principals’ responses to the 11 items were combined to create the School discipline problems scale. The 3 
categories of the scale are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups 
based on their principals’ scale scores:
	
z on average, the hardly any problems category for Year 4 students corresponds to principals who reported 
not a problem for 5 of the 10 issues and minor problem for the other 5 issues 
	
z on average, the moderate to severe problems category for Year 4 students corresponds to principals who 
reported moderate problems for 5 of the 10 issues and minor problem for the other 5 issues
	
z on average, the hardly any problems category for Year 8 students corresponds to principals who reported 
not a problem for 6 of the 11 issues and minor problem for the other 5 issues
	
z on average, the moderate to severe problems category for Year 8 students corresponds to principals who 
reported moderate problems for 6 of the 11 issues and minor problem for the other 5 issues
	
z all other Year 4 and Year 8 students attended schools with minor problems.

FIGURE 3.5 The School discipline problems scale and student achievement in mathematics and science, Australia and

Year 4 Australian students
	
z 51% attended schools whose principals reported hardly any problems with school discipline compared to 
the international average of 58%.
	
z 43% attended schools whose principals reported minor problems compared to the international average 
of 32%.
	
z 6% attended schools whose principals reported moderate to severe problems compared to the

Year 8 Australian students
	
z 31% attended schools whose principals reported hardly any problems with school discipline compared to

z 57% attended schools with minor problems compared to the international average of 45%.
	
z 13% attended schools with moderate to severe problems with school discipline compared to the 
international average of 14%.

36
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Figure 3.5 shows there is a small relationship between students’ achievement and principals’ reports 
of school disciplinary problems; fewer disciplinary problems are associated with higher achievement (a 
correlation of around 0.20 at Year 4 and 0.27 at Year 8). The difference between the average achievement 
of Australian students who attended schools whose principals reported hardly any problems with school 
discipline and students at schools with moderate to severe problems with school discipline was between 56 
points for Year 8 science and 76 points for Year 4 mathematics.
The differences between all groups were statistically significant for both subjects at both year levels. 
Internationally, on average, the gap between hardly any problems and moderate to severe problems was 
around 35 points at Year 4 and around 40 points at Year 8.
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 3.6 presents the percentages of Australian students in each of the 3 categories of the School 
discipline problems scale, according to school socioeconomic composition.
At Year 4, there were larger proportions of students who attended more affluent schools and students at 
neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools who had principals who reported hardly any problems 
with school discipline, compared to the proportion of students who attended more disadvantaged schools. 
There were smaller proportions of students who had principals who reported minor problems with school 
discipline at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools and more affluent schools compared to 
more disadvantaged schools.
There were smaller proportions of students who had principals who reported moderate to severe problems 
with school discipline at more affluent schools compared to more disadvantaged schools.
At Year 8, there were larger proportions of students who attended more affluent schools and students at 
neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools who had principals who reported hardly any problems 
with school discipline compared to the proportion of students who attended more disadvantaged schools. 
There were smaller proportions of students who had principals who reported minor problems with school 
discipline at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools and more affluent schools compared to 
more disadvantaged schools.
There were smaller proportions of students who had principals who reported moderate to severe problems 
with school discipline at more affluent schools compared to more disadvantaged schools.

3.5	
Teachers’ reports of safe and orderly schools
Teachers were asked about the extent of their agreement with the following statements (agree a lot; agree a 
little; disagree a little; or disagree a lot):
	
z I feel safe at this school.
	
z This school’s security policies and practices are sufficient.
	
z The students behave in an orderly manner.
	
z The students are respectful of the teachers.
	
z The students respect school property.
	
z This school has clear rules about student conduct.
	
z This school’s rules are enforced in a fair and consistent manner.
Teachers’ responses to the 7 items were combined to create the Safe and orderly school scale. The 3 
categories of the scale are comprised in the following way and all students were assigned to these based on 
their teachers’ scale scores. 
	
z on average, the very safe and orderly category corresponds to teachers agreeing a lot with 4 of the 7 
qualities and agreeing a little with the other 3 qualities.
	
z on average, the less than safe and orderly category corresponds to teachers disagreeing a little with 4 of 
the 7 qualities and agreeing a little with the other 3 qualities.
	
z All other students attended somewhat safe and orderly schools.
Figure 3.7 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
mathematics and science scores.

Safe and orderly school environment
FIGURE 3.7 The Safe and orderly school scale and student achievement in mathematics and science, Australia and 
the international averages 

38
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 52% had mathematics teachers and 49% had science teachers who reported that their schools were very 
safe and orderly.
	
z 42% had mathematics teachers and 44% had science teachers who reported that their schools were 
somewhat safe and orderly.
	
z 6% had mathematics teachers and 7% had science teachers who reported their schools were less than 
safe and orderly. 
These percentages were all similar to the international averages.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 37% had mathematics teachers and 35% had science teachers who reported that their schools were very 
safe and orderly compared to the international average of 43%.
	
z 54% had mathematics teachers and 49% had science teachers who reported that their schools were 
somewhat safe and orderly compared to the international average of 50%.
	
z 10% had mathematics teachers and 16% had science teachers who reported that their schools were less 
than safe and orderly compared to the international average of 7% and 8%, respectively.
Figure 3.7 shows there is a small relationship between students’ achievement and teachers’ reports about 
the safety and orderliness of their schools in Australia; more safe and orderly schools are associated with 
higher achievement (a correlation of around 0.17 at Year 4 and 0.20 at Year 8). 
The difference between the average achievement of Australian Year 4 students whose teachers reported that 
their schools were very safe and orderly and students whose teachers reported that their schools were less 
than safe and orderly was 61 points for mathematics and 46 for science. 
The difference between the average achievement of Australian Year 8 students whose teachers reported 
that their schools were very safe and orderly and students whose teachers reported that their schools were 
less than safe and orderly was 45 points for mathematics and 44 for science. The differences between all 
groups were statistically significant for both subjects at both year levels, except for between Australian 
Year 4 students whose teachers reported that their schools were very safe and orderly and students whose 
teachers reported that their schools were somewhat safe and orderly for mathematics, and between 
Australian Year 8 students whose teachers reported that their schools were somewhat safe and orderly and 
students whose teachers reported that their schools were less than safe and orderly for mathematics and 
science.
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 3.8 presents the proportions of Australian students in each of the 3 categories of the Safe and orderly 
school scale, according to school socioeconomic composition. It shows that, at both Year 4 and Year 8, 
students who attended more affluent schools were more likely to have teachers who reported that their 
schools were very safe and orderly, and less likely to have teachers who reported that their schools were less 
than safe and orderly than students who attended more disadvantaged schools.
For Year 4 mathematics, there were significant differences between students in more disadvantaged schools 
and those attending more affluent or neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools in terms of the 
percentages of students who had mathematics teachers who reported that their schools were very safe and 
orderly and those who reported that their schools were less than safe and orderly. 
For Year 4 science, only the percentage of students who had science teachers who reported that their 
schools were very safe and orderly was significantly different between students in more disadvantaged 
schools and those at more affluent schools.

Chapter 3 School climate, discipline and safety
39
For Year 8 mathematics, there were significant differences between students in more disadvantaged 
schools and those at more affluent or neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools in terms of 
the percentage of students who had mathematics teachers who reported that their schools were very safe 
and orderly. The percentage of students whose mathematics teachers reported that their schools were 
somewhat safe and orderly was also significantly different between students in more disadvantaged schools 
and those at more affluent schools. 
For Year 8 science, there were significant differences in the percentage of students who had science 
teachers who reported that their schools were very safe and orderly between all 3 socioeconomic groups. 
In addition, there were significant differences between students in more affluent schools and those at more 
disadvantaged or neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools in terms of the percentages of 
students who had mathematics teachers who reported that their schools were less than safe and orderly.

3.6	
Students’ reports of bullying
Students’ views about their personal safety at school were collected using items that focused on their 
experience of bullying behaviours.
Year 4 students were asked to indicate how often other students had done any of the following to them 
(never; a few times a year; once or twice a month; or at least once a week):
	
z made fun of me or called me names
	
z left me out of their games or activities
	
z spread lies about me
	
z stole something from me
	
z damaged something of mine on purpose
	
z hit or hurt me (e.g. shoved, hit, kicked)
	
z made me do things I didn’t want to do
	
z sent me nasty or hurtful messages online
	
z shared nasty or hurtful messages about me online
	
z shared embarrassing photos of me online
	
z threatened me.
Students’ responses to these 11 items were combined to create the Student bullying scale. The 3 categories 
are comprised in the following wasy and all students were assigned to these based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, the never or almost never group corresponds to students who never experienced 6 of the 11 
bullying behaviours and experienced 5 of the other behaviours a few times a year
	
z on average, the about weekly group corresponds to students who experienced 6 of the 11 behaviours once 
or twice a month and the other 5 a few times a year
	
z all other students were in the monthly group.
Year 8 students were asked to indicate how often other students had done any of the following to them 
(never; a few times a year; once or twice a month; or at least once a week):
	
z said mean things about my physical appearance (e.g. my hair, my size)
	
z spread lies about me
	
z shared my secrets with others
	
z refused to talk to me
	
z said hurtful things to or about me because of my cultural background (e.g. race, ethnicity, religion)
	
z stolen something from me
	
z made me do things I didn’t want to do
	
z sent me nasty or hurtful messages online
	
z shared nasty or hurtful messages about me online
	
z shared embarrassing photos of me online
	
z threatened me
	
z physically hurt me
	
z excluded me from their group (e.g. parties, messaging)
	
z damaged something of mine on purpose.

Chapter 3 School climate, discipline and safety
41
Students’ responses to these 14 items were combined to create the Student bullying scale. The 3 categories 
are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, the never or almost never group corresponds to students who never experienced 7 of the 14 
bullying behaviours and experienced 7 of the other behaviours a few times a year
	
z on average, the about weekly group corresponds to students who experienced 7of the 14 behaviours once 
or twice a month and the other 7 a few times a year
	
z all other students were in the monthly group.
Figure 3.9 presents the proportions of students in each of these categories, along with their average 
mathematics and science scores.

Frequency of bullying at school
FIGURE 3.9 The Student bullying scale and student achievement in mathematics and science, Australia and the 
international averages 

42
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 48% reported experiencing these behaviours never or almost never compared to the international average 
of 53%.
	
z 39% reported experiencing these behaviours about monthly compared to the international average of 31%.
	
z 13% reported experiencing these behaviours about weekly compared to the international average of 15%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 48% reported experiencing these behaviours never or almost never compared to the international average 
of 60%.
	
z 36% reported experiencing these behaviours about monthly compared to the international average of 27%.
	
z 16% reported experiencing these behaviours about weekly compared to the international average of 13%.
Figure 3.9 shows, there is a small association between achievement and Australian students’ experience of 
bullying behaviours, with a lower experience of bullying behaviours associated with higher achievement (a 
correlation of around 0.17 at Year 4 and 0.29 at Year 8). The difference between the average achievement 
of Australian students who reported experiencing these behaviours never or almost never and the average 
achievement of students who reported experiencing these behaviours about weekly was between 44 and 55 
points for both subjects at both year levels. 
The differences between all groups were statistically significant for both subjects at both year levels. 
Internationally, on average, the gap between experiencing bullying behaviours never or almost never and 
experiencing these behaviours about weekly was between 58 points for Year 8 mathematics and 68 points 
for Year 8 science.
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 3.10 presents the percentages of Australian students in each of the 3 categories of the Student 
bullying scale, according to school socioeconomic composition.  
At both Year 4 and Year 8, there were statistically significant differences between the socioeconomic groups 
in the never or almost never and about weekly categories. 
That is, there was a larger proportion of students at more affluent schools who experienced bullying 
behaviours never or almost never compared to students at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged 
schools and at more disadvantaged schools. Conversely, a lower proportion of students at more affluent 
schools experienced bullying behaviours about weekly compared to students at neither more affluent nor 
more disadvantaged schools and students at more disadvantaged schools.

3.7	
Student absenteeism
Students were asked how often they were absent from school (never or almost never; once every 2 months; 
once a month; once every 2 weeks; or once a week).
Figure 3.11 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students according to how often they 
were absent from school, along with their average achievement in mathematics and science.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 54% were never or almost never absent from school compared to the international average of 55%.
	
z 16% were absent once every 2 months compared to the international average of 13%.
	
z 15% were absent once a month compared to the international average of 12%.
	
z 7% were absent once every 2 weeks, which was the same as the international average.
	
z 7% were absent once a week compared to the international average of 13%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 35% were never or almost never absent from school compared to the international average of 46%.
	
z 20% were absent once every 2 months compared to the international average of 16%.
	
z 22% were absent once a month compared to the international average of 16%.
	
z 15% were absent once every 2 weeks compared to the international average of 10%.
	
z 8% were absent once a week compared to the international average of 12%.
Figure 3.11 shows that at Year 4, Australian students who were never or almost never absent scored around 
87 points higher in mathematics and around 73 points higher in science than those who were absent once 
a week. Australian Year 8 students who were never or almost never absent scored over 80 points higher in 
mathematics and 84 points higher in science than those who were absent once a week. Internationally, on 
average, this gap was over 60 points at Year 4 and around 80 points at Year 8.
In Australia, all differences between groups were significant, except for between once a month and once every 
two months, and once every two months and never or almost never for Year 4 mathematics; between once 
every two months and never or almost never, and once a month and never or almost never for Year 4 science; 
and between once every two months and never or almost never for Year 8 mathematics and science.

FIGURE 3.11 Frequency of student absences and student achievement in mathematics and science, Australia and the 
international averages
Socioeconomic differences
Figure 3.12 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students according to how often they 
were absent from school, by school socioeconomic composition. 
At Year 4, there were statistically significant differences in the number of students who reported being 
absent once a week, across all groups, with the proportion being higher for students in more disadvantaged 
schools compared to neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools, which was higher than that for 
students in more affluent schools. There was also a higher proportion of students in more disadvantaged 
schools and neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools who reported being absent once every 
two weeks compared to students in more affluent schools. Conversely, a higher proportion of students 
from more affluent schools reported being absent once every two months compared to students in more 
disadvantaged schools.
At Year 8, there were statistically significant differences in the frequency of student absences between 
students from more affluent schools and neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools compared 
to students from more disadvantaged schools in terms of the proportions of students who reported being 
absent once a week and also those students who reported being absent once every two months.

Students feel tired 
Students were asked how often they arrived at school feeling tired (never; sometimes; every day; or almost 
every day). 
Figure 3.13 presents these data for Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, along with average achievement in 
mathematics and science.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 8% reported never arriving tired at school compared to the international average of 14%.
	
z 44% reported sometimes arriving at school tired compared to the international average of 42%.
	
z 48% reported arriving at school tired every day or almost every day compared to the international average 
of 44%.
The average mathematics scores of Australian Year 4 students who never or only sometimes arrived at 
school tired were significantly higher than that of students who arrived at school tired every day or almost 
every day. 
Whereas the average science scores of Australian students who sometimes arrived at school tired was 
significantly higher than students who never arrived at school tired and who arrived at school tired every day 
or almost every day. 
Internationally, the average mathematics and science scores were significantly different between all groups 
at Year 4.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 4% reported never arriving tired at school compared to the international average of 7%.
	
z 35% reported sometimes arriving tired at school compared to the international average of 38%.
	
z 61% reported arriving at school tired every day or almost every day compared to the international average 
of 55%.

Students feel hungry 
Students were also asked how often they arrived at school feeling hungry (never; sometimes; every day; or

Year 4 Australian students
	
z 20% reported never arriving at school feeling hungry compared to the international average of 23%
	
z 43% reported sometimes arriving at school feeling hungry compared to the international average of 38%

z 20% reported never arriving at school feeling hungry which was the same as the international average

z 34% reported arriving at school feeling hungry every day or almost every day compared to the international

In Australia, in both Year 4 and Year 8, there was a relationship between how frequently students arrived 
at school feeling hungry and their average mathematics achievement. Australian students who reported 
arriving at school feeling hungry every day or almost every day scored significantly lower, on average, than 
students who never arrived at school feeling hungry by between 44 points for Year 4 mathematics and 28 
points for Year 4 science. At Year 8, the difference was 39 points for mathematics and 40 points for science.

CHAPTER 4
MATHEMATICS TEACHER PREPARATION, 
PROFESSIONAL DEVELOPMENT AND 
JOB SATISFACTION
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 4 for detailed analysis.
	
z While almost all students were taught mathematics by teachers with at least a bachelor’s degree or 
equivalent, only 21% of Year 4 students and 34% of Year 8 students were taught by teachers with a 
postgraduate university degree, compared to 33% of Year 4 students and 39% of Year 8 students, on 
average, internationally.
	
z At Year 4, 83% of students were taught mathematics by teachers who majored in primary education, and 
a further 12% were taught mathematics by teachers who majored in mathematics as well as primary 
education.
	
z At Year 8, 76% of students were taught mathematics by teachers who majored in either or both 
mathematics and mathematics education, with the remaining 24% being taught by mathematics 
teachers who had not majored in either mathematics or mathematics education, and who could be 
considered to be teaching out-of-field.
	
z Mathematics teachers were asked about the professional development they had taken in the previous 
two years. At both Year 4 and Year 8, the most common topic was mathematics curriculum.
	
z Mathematics teachers were asked about their need for future professional development opportunities in 
different topic areas. At both Year 4 and Year 8, the most popular topic was improving students’ critical 
thinking or problem-solving skills.
	
z 44% of Year 4 students and 47% of Year 8 students were taught by mathematics teachers who reported 
that they were very satisfied with their careers. There were no significant differences in mathematics 
achievement scores according to the level of teacher job satisfaction.

Chapter 4 Mathematics teacher preparation, professional development and job satisfaction
51
4.1	
Introduction
While many factors affect learning and achievement, according to the OECD, ‘the single most important 
factor within schools is teaching and quality of teachers and instructors’ (OECD, n.d.). Chapters 4 to 7 of 
this report focus on the characteristics and job-related experiences of students’ mathematics and science 
teachers (in Chapters 4 and 6) and also teaching practices and the classroom experiences of both teachers 
and students (in Chapters 5 and 7).
Many teacher characteristics can affect student learning. These include teachers’ education and levels of 
experience (Darling-Hammond, 2017; Podolsky & Kini, 2019). In addition, job-related experiences, such as 
teachers’ access to high-quality professional development and job satisfaction have also been found to 
impact on student learning (Darling-Hammond et al., 2017; Johnson et al., 2012).
This chapter focuses on the characteristics and job-related experiences of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 
students’ mathematics teachers and draws on data collected in the TIMSS 2023 teacher questionnaire. 
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, results from the teacher questionnaire and the school questionnaire 
are presented with regard to students. This means that each result is either reported as the percentage of 
students who attended schools that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were 
taught by teachers who responded in a particular way. Please see the Reader’s guide for more information.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 4 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors. 
4.2	
Mathematics teachers’ qualifications
Bachelor’s and postgraduate degrees
Table 4.1 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students according to their mathematics teachers’ 
higher education qualification levels. The table shows the results for Australia and compares these with the 
international averages.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 77% were taught mathematics by teachers who had completed a bachelor’s degree or equivalent but not a 
postgraduate degree compared to the international average of 58%.
	
z 21% had mathematics teachers with a postgraduate university degree (i.e. doctorate, master’s or other 
postgraduate degree) compared to the international average of 33%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 66% of Australian students were taught mathematics by teachers who had completed a bachelor’s degree 
or equivalent compared to the international average of 57%.
	
z 34% of students had mathematics teachers with a postgraduate university degree compared to the 
international average of 39%.

Tertiary education majors or specialisation
In addition to reporting on teachers’ levels of education, the teacher questionnaire also investigated the 
major(s) or specialisation(s) that teachers undertook during their tertiary studies. Figure 4.1 presents these 
data. It shows the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students with mathematics teachers who 
studied different types of major or specialisation related to mathematics teaching.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 12% had mathematics teachers with a major in primary education and mathematics compared to the 
international average of 35%.
	
z 83% had teachers with a major in primary education but not mathematics, compared to the international 
average of 41%.
	
z 1% had teachers with a major or specialisation in mathematics but not primary education compared with 
the international average of 12%.
	
z 5% had teachers who had other majors compared to the international average of 9%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 46% had mathematics teachers with a major in mathematics and mathematics education compared to the 
international average of 42%.
	
z 16% were taught by teachers with a major in mathematics education but not mathematics compared to the 
international average of 13%.
	
z 15% had teachers who had a major in mathematics but not mathematics education, which was lower than 
the international average of 35%.
	
z 24% had mathematics teachers who had other majors compared to the international average of 9%.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 4.2 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students according to the type of 
major or specialisation of their mathematics teachers but also presents these data according to the 
socioeconomic composition of the students’ schools. 
At Year 4, there were no significant differences in the percentages of students taught by teachers according 
to the type of major or specialisation of their mathematics teachers across the socioeconomic groups, 
except for the teachers with other majors. The proportion of students from neither more affluent nor more 
disadvantaged schools had a higher percentage of mathematics teachers who had mathematics teachers 
with other majors than students in more affluent schools and this difference was statistically significant. 
For Year 8, there were no significant differences in the percentages of students taught by teachers according 
to the type of major or specialisation of their mathematics teachers across the socioeconomic groups due to 
large standard errors.

4.4	
Mathematics teachers’ participation in professional development 
Many education systems, including Australia’s, require registered teachers to participate in ongoing 
professional development (PD) – supplementary to their initial qualifications – to ensure that students 
benefit from up-to-date instruction methods and information. This was investigated in the teacher 
questionnaire. 
Figure 4.4 presents the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students whose mathematics teachers reported 
participating in various forms of PD in the past 2 years. Teachers’ attendance at PD is influenced by multiple 
factors such as the availability of courses to teachers and schools, school funding, school or systemic 
requirements as well as opportunity or time and teacher interest.
From the types of professional development listed in the teacher questionnaire, Australian Year 4 and Year 8 
students had mathematics teachers who reported participation in the following PD in the past 2 years:
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 61% mathematics curriculum 
	
z 59% mathematics content 
	
z 56% mathematics pedagogy/instruction 
	
z 55% addressing individual students’ needs 
	
z 46% improving students’ critical thinking or problem-solving skills 
	
z 43% mathematics assessment 
	
z 23% integrating technology into mathematics instruction.
The percentages of students whose mathematics teachers attended PD were significantly higher in Australia 
compared to the international average, except for improving students’ critical thinking or problem-solving 
skills and mathematics assessment, which were not significantly different in Australia compared to the 
international average; and the topic of integrating technology into mathematics instruction, which was 
significantly lower in Australia. 

58
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 73% mathematics curriculum 
	
z 63% addressing individual students’ needs 
	
z 59% mathematics content 
	
z 58% mathematics pedagogy/instruction
	
z 47% improving students’ critical thinking or problem-solving skills 
	
z 42% integrating information technology into mathematics 
	
z 38% mathematics assessment.
Internationally, the percentages of students who had mathematics teachers who attended PD on any 
topic were similar to Australia. However, the percentages of students who had mathematics teachers who 
attended PD in mathematics curriculum and addressing individual students’ needs were substantially and 
significantly higher in Australia (by 22 and 19 percentage points, respectively), while integrating information 
technology into mathematics and mathematics assessment were significantly lower in Australia than 
internationally.

4.5	
Mathematics teachers’ professional development needs
To complement data on participation in PD, the teacher questionnaire also asked teachers about their need 
for future PD opportunities in different topic areas.
Figure 4.5 presents the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students whose mathematics teachers reported 
needing various forms of PD in the future. These data were less likely to be influenced by factors that might 
have impacted their previous attendance at PD (e.g. funding and availability etc.) and were more likely to 
reflect teachers’ interest in furthering their knowledge and understanding on particular topics.

Chapter 4 Mathematics teacher preparation, professional development and job satisfaction
59
The format of the teacher questionnaire allows teachers to respond freely to both questions, assessing 
teachers’ attended-PD as well future-PD needs. This means that a teacher could have responded that they 
attended PD on a particular topic in the last 2 years and then also responded that they wanted to do further 
training on this same topic in the future.
Year 4 mathematics teachers
The future PD topics, for students taught by Year 4 mathematics teachers, in order of most to least desired 
were:
	
z 81% improving students critical thinking or problem-solving
	
z 78% integrating technology into mathematics
	
z 68% addressing individual student needs
	
z 65% mathematics assessment
	
z 62% mathematics pedagogy/instruction
	
z 55% mathematics curriculum
	
z 54% mathematics content. 
In general, the percentages of Australian students being taught by mathematics teachers who requested 
PD on specific areas was significantly higher than the international averages, particularly with improving 
students’ critical thinking or problem-solving, where 81% of Australian students’ teachers expressed a 
need for PD compared with the international average of 65%. The only exception was addressing individual 
student needs which was not significantly different in Australia compared with the international average.
For some topics, there was a notably higher percentage of Australian Year 4 students who were taught by 
teachers who reported needing PD on that topic compared to the percentage of students being taught by 
teachers who participated in that topic over the past 2 years. The greatest differences were for the topics of 
integrating technology into mathematics (a 55 percentage-point difference) and improving students’ critical 
thinking or problem-solving skills (a 35 percentage-point difference). 
Year 8 mathematics teachers
The future PD topics, for students taught by Year 8 mathematics teachers, in order of most to least desired 
were:
	
z 73% improving students’ critical thinking or problem-solving skills
	
z 63% addressing individual student needs
	
z 61% integrating technology into mathematics instruction
	
z 57% mathematics assessment
	
z 56% mathematics pedagogy/instruction
	
z 56% mathematics curriculum
	
z 43% mathematics content.
There was little variation between the percentages of Australian students being taught by mathematics 
teachers who requested PD on specific areas and the international averages.
At Year 8, across most topics, the percentage of Australian students who were taught by mathematics 
teachers who reported needing PD on that topic was less than the percentage of students being taught 
by teachers who participated in that topic over the past 2 years. However, for 2 topics the percentage of 
Australian students who were taught by mathematics teachers who reported needing PD on that topic was 
greater than the percentage of students being taught by teachers who participated in that topic over the past 
two years: improving students’ critical thinking or problem-solving skills (a 26 percentage-point difference) 
and mathematics assessment (a 19 percentage-point difference).

4.6	
Mathematics teachers’ job satisfaction 
Teachers’ satisfaction with their careers can impact their wellbeing and students’ attitudes towards school 
and learning. Job satisfaction can also be associated with teacher efficacy and therefore is an area of 
interest when investigating teacher effectiveness.
Teachers were asked to indicate how often they agreed with the following statements (very often; often; 
sometimes; or never or almost never):
	
z I am content with my profession as a teacher.
	
z I find my work full of meaning and purpose.
	
z I am enthusiastic about my job.
	
z My work inspires me.
	
z I am proud of the work I do.
	
z I enjoy the challenges of teaching.
Teachers’ responses to these 6 items were combined to create the Teacher job satisfaction scale. The 
categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups based on 
their teachers’ scale scores:
	
z on average, very satisfied mathematics teachers corresponds to teachers who responded very often to 
3 of the 6 statements and often to the other 3 statements
	
z on average, less than satisfied mathematics teachers corresponds to teachers who responded sometimes 
to 3 of the 6 statements and often to the other 3 statements 
	
z all other students had somewhat satisfied teachers.

Chapter 4 Mathematics teacher preparation, professional development and job satisfaction
61
Figure 4.6 presents the proportions of students in Year 4 and Year 8 for each of these categories, according 
to their mathematics teachers’ responses, along with their average mathematics scores.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 44% were taught by mathematics teachers who reported they were very satisfied with their careers 
compared to the international average of 60%.
	
z 40% were taught by mathematics teachers who reported they were somewhat satisfied compared to the 
international average of 32%.
	
z 15% were taught by mathematics teachers who reported they were less than satisfied compared to the 
international average of 8%.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 47% were taught by mathematics teachers who reported they were very satisfied with their careers 
compared to the international average of 55%.
	
z 37% were taught by mathematics teachers who reported they were somewhat satisfied compared to the 
international average of 35%.
	
z 17% were taught by mathematics teachers who reported they were less than satisfied compared to the 
international average of 10%.
There were no significant differences found between mathematics achievement scores according to the 
level of job satisfaction reported by students’ teachers for Australia at Year 4 and Year 8.

CHAPTER 5
TEACHING MATHEMATICS
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 5 for detailed analysis.
	
z 86% of Year 4 students and 82% of Year 8 students had been taught all of the mathematics topics 
before or during the relevant year level, compared to the international average of 78% (at both year 
levels). At Year 4, all sub-domains had a coverage of over 80%. At Year 8, all sub-domains except for 
algebra had a coverage of about 80% of students; algebra was lower at 58% of students. Internationally, 
69% of students had been taught the algebra topics before or during Year 8.
	
z 68% of Year 4 students and 39% of Year 8 students reported high clarity of instruction in their 
mathematics lessons. These students had higher achievement in mathematics than those who reported 
low clarity of instruction.
	
z Approximately 25% of Year 4 and Year 8 students reported disorderly behaviour in most mathematics 
lessons. Students who reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons had higher mathematics 
achievement than students who reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons.
	
z While there were no significant differences between socioeconomic groups at Year 8, a higher 
percentage of Year 4 students who attended more disadvantaged schools than more affluent schools 
reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons.
	
z Approximately 17% of Year 4 and Year 8 students were taught mathematics by teachers who reported 
that their teaching was limited very little by students not ready for instruction, with around 71-75% 
taught by mathematics teachers who reported that their teaching was limited some. Students whose 
mathematics teachers reported that their teaching was limited very little had higher achievement in 
mathematics than students whose mathematics teachers reported that their teaching was limited a lot.
	
z There was a higher percentage of Year 4 students who attended more affluent schools than more 
disadvantaged schools who had mathematics teachers that reported their teaching was limited very 
little by students not ready for instruction. There were no significant differences by socioeconomic 
composition of the school at Year 8.

64
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
5.1	
Introduction
Various factors in the classroom can support or impede the teaching and learning of mathematics. These 
include practical elements such as the amount of instructional time devoted to mathematics, or the coverage 
of the domain (Schmidt et al., 2015). Other factors include the use of teaching strategies that lead to 
instructional clarity, or student factors such as disorderly behaviour or other limitations (Nilsen et al., 2016; 
van Dijk et al., 2019). 
This chapter focuses on the teaching practices and factors in the classroom that can support or obstruct 
learning in mathematics and draws on data collected in the TIMSS 2023 student questionnaire and the 
teacher questionnaire.
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, results from the teacher questionnaire and the school questionnaire 
are presented with regard to students. This means that each result is either reported as the percentage of 
students who attended a school that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were 
taught by teachers who responded in a particular way. Please see the Reader’s guide for more information.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 5 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors. 
5.2	
Instructional time in mathematics
Instructional time in mathematics classes is likely to impact on students’ mathematical learning. Using 
TIMSS data derived from teachers’ reports of weekly instructional time for mathematics and principals’ 
reports of how many days the school is open for instruction (weekly and yearly), it was possible to estimate 
the average hours per year that Australian students received in mathematics instruction.
Year 4 mathematics instruction
The average time for Year 4 maths instruction in Australia was 198 hours per year. The international average 
was 179 hours per year. The average annual hours of instruction time varied among other countries that 
participated in TIMSS 2023:
Highest hours 
	
z Belgium (French), 263 hours 
	
z Portugal, 258 hours
	
z Saudi Arabia, 246 hours
	
z Italy, 242 hours.
Lowest hours 
	
z Albania, 112 hours
	
z Bosnia and Herzegovina, 
112 hours
	
z Korea, 103 hours.
Comparison countries
	
z Canada, 195 hours 
	
z England, 199 hours 
	
z Ireland, 165 hours 
	
z New Zealand, 166 hours 
	
z Singapore, 203 hours 
	
z United States, 209 hours.

Chapter 5 Teaching mathematics
65
Year 8 mathematics instruction
The average time for Year 8 maths instruction in Australia was 138 hours per year. The international average 
was 149 hours per year. The average annual hours of instruction time varied among other countries that 
participated in TIMSS 2023:
Highest hours
	
z Saudi Arabia, 224 hours
	
z Oman, 196 hours
	
z South Africa, 191 hours 
	
z Chile, 190 hours
	
z United Arab Emirates, 
186 hours. 
Lowest hours 
	
z Ireland, 105 hours
	
z Japan, 107 hours
	
z Korea, 103 hours.
Comparison countries
	
z England, 129 hours 
	
z Ireland, 105 hours 
	
z Singapore, 128 hours 
	
z United States, 152 hours.
5.3	
Coverage of TIMSS mathematics topics 
Teachers were asked whether the students in the participating TIMSS class had been taught each of 
the TIMSS topics mostly before or during the year of assessment. Table 5.1 shows the TIMSS topics for 
mathematics at both Year 4 and Year 8 levels according to the content domains. Figure 5.1 shows the 
percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students who had been taught the TIMSS topics, on average, for each 
content domain. The figure shows the results for Australia and the international averages.
Year 4 Australian students
Eighty-six per cent of Australian Year 4 students had been taught all of the TIMSS mathematics topics before 
or during Year 4, compared to the international average of 78%. Across the domains:
	
z data 93% compared to the international average of 78%
	
z number 84%, compared to the international average of 85%
	
z measurement and geometry 83% compared to the international average of 74%.
Year 8 Australian students
Eighty-two per cent of Australian Year 8 students had been taught all of the TIMSS mathematics topics 
before or during Year 4, compared to the international average of 78%. Across the domains:
	
z number 97%, which was the same as the international average
	
z data and probability 90% compared to the international average of 72%
	
z geometry and measurement 81% compared to the international average of 78%
	
z algebra 58% compared to the international average of 69%.

5.4	
Instructional clarity in mathematics lessons
The degree to which teachers are clear in the way they deliver the mathematics curriculum is likely to 
influence student learning. Students were asked to indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot, agree a little, 
disagree a little or disagree a lot) with each of the following statements about instructional clarity in their 
mathematics lessons:
	
z My teacher makes it clear what we should learn in each lesson.
	
z My teacher is easy to understand.
	
z My teacher has clear answers to my questions.
	
z My teacher is good at explaining mathematics.
	
z My teacher does a variety of things to help us learn.
	
z My teacher explains a topic again when we don’t understand.
	
z My teacher gives me helpful feedback on my work.
Students’ responses to these 7 items were combined to create the Instructional clarity in mathematics 
lessons scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these 
groups based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, high clarity corresponds to students who responded agree a lot to 4 of the 7 statements and 
agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z on average, low clarity corresponds to students who responded disagree a little to 4 of the 7 statements 
and agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z all other students reported moderate clarity of instruction in their mathematics lessons. 
Figure 5.2 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in the clarity of instruction 
categories, along with their average achievement in mathematics.

z 39% reported moderate clarity of instruction compared to the international average of 36%.
	
z 22% reported low clarity of instruction compared to the international average of 15%. 
Figure 5.2 illustrates a pattern between reported clarity of instruction in the mathematics classroom and 
mathematics achievement. 
At Year 4, Australian students who reported high clarity or moderate clarity of instruction in their 
mathematics lessons had significantly higher mathematics achievement than students who reported low 
clarity of instruction.
At Year 8, Australian students who reported high clarity of instruction in their mathematics lessons had 
significantly higher mathematics achievement than students who reported moderate clarity of instruction. 
Both students reporting high clarity and those reporting moderate clarity of instruction had significantly 
higher mathematics achievement than students who reported low clarity of instruction.

percentage of students in each instructional clarity category were similar, with no significant differences, 
across all 3 socioeconomic groups.

5.5	
Disorderly behaviour during mathematics lessons
Disorderly behaviour in class can disrupt student learning. Students were asked about the frequency of the 
following things taking place during their mathematics lessons (never, some lessons, about half the lessons 
or every or almost every lesson):
	
z Students don’t listen to what the teacher says.
	
z There is too much noise for students to work well.
	
z My teacher has to wait a long time for students to be quiet.
	
z Students interrupt the teacher.
	
z Students do not follow the classroom rules.
	
z Other students’ behaviour makes it hard for me to concentrate.
Students’ responses to these 6 items were combined to create the Disorderly behaviour during mathematics 
lessons scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these 
groups based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, students who reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons corresponds to students who 
responded never to 3 of the 6 statements and some lessons to the other 3 statements
	
z on average, students who reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons corresponds to students who 
responded in every or almost every lesson to 3 of the 6 statements and about half the lessons to the other 
3 statements
	
z all other students reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons.
Figure 5.4 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of these categories, 
along with their average achievement in mathematics.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 5% reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons compared to the international average of 16%.
	
z 70% reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons compared to the international average of 65%.
	
z 24% reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons compared to the international average of 19%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 10% reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons compared to the international average of 22%. 
	
z 66% reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons compared to the international average of 61%.
	
z 25% reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons compared to the international average of 17%. 
Figure 5.4 illustrates a pattern between reported disorderly behaviour in mathematics lessons and 
mathematics achievement. 
Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students who reported experiencing disorderly behaviour in only a few or no 
lessons had significantly higher mathematics achievement than those reporting disorderly behaviour in 
some lessons. And both those students who reported disorderly behaviour in only a few or no lessons or in 
some lessons had higher mathematics achievement than those who reported disorderly behaviour in most 
lessons.

5.6	
Mathematics teaching limited by students not ready for instruction
Teachers of the Year 4 and Year 8 classes that undertook TIMSS 2023 were asked about the extent to which 
instruction at their school was limited (a lot, some or not at all) by the following student attributes:
	
z students lacking prerequisite knowledge or skills
	
z students suffering from lack of basic nutrition
	
z students suffering from not enough sleep
	
z students absent from class
	
z disruptive students
	
z uninterested students
	
z distracted students
	
z students with mental, emotional or psychological impairment
	
z students with difficulties understanding the language of instruction.
Teachers’ responses to these 9 items were combined to create the Classroom teaching limited by students 
not ready for instruction scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were 
assigned to these groups based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, students with mathematics teachers who felt their teaching was limited very little corresponds 
to teachers who responded not at all to 5 of the 9 attributes and some to the other 4 attributes
	
z on average, students with mathematics teachers who felt their teaching was limited a lot corresponds to 
teachers who responded a lot to 5 of the 9 attributes and some to the other 4 attributes
	
z all other students had mathematics teachers who felt their teaching was limited some.
Figure 5.6 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of these categories, 
along with their average achievement in mathematics.

CHAPTER 6
SCIENCE TEACHER PREPARATION, 
PROFESSIONAL DEVELOPMENT AND 
JOB SATISFACTION
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 6 for detailed analysis.
	
z While almost all Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students were taught science by teachers with at least a 
bachelor’s degree or equivalent, only 21% of Year 4 students and 38% of Year 8 students were taught by 
teachers with a postgraduate university degree, compared to the international averages of 34% and 41%, 
respectively.
	
z At Year 4, 83% of students were taught science by teachers who majored in primary education, with a 
further 9% taught science by teachers who majored in science as well as primary education.
	
z At Year 8, 94% of students were taught science by teachers who majored in either or both of science 
and science education, with the remaining 6% being taught by science teachers who had not majored in 
either science or science education, and who could be considered to be teaching out-of-field.
	
z Science teachers were asked about the professional development they had taken over the previous two 
years. At both Year 4 and Year 8, the most common topic was assessing individual students’ needs.
	
z Students’ science teachers were asked about their need for future professional development 
opportunities in different topic areas. At Year 4, the most popular topic was integrating technology into 
science instruction. At Year 8, it was improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills.
	
z 46% of Year 4 students and 36% of Year 8 students were taught by science teachers who reported that 
they were very satisfied with their careers. There were no significant differences in science achievement 
scores according to the level of job satisfaction reported by students’ teachers.

74
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
6.1	
Introduction
Like Chapters 4 and 5, Chapters 6 and 7 consider factors associated with effective science teaching. This 
chapter focuses on the characteristics and experiences of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students’ science 
teachers, and draws on data collected in the teacher questionnaire. Chapter 7 explores the teaching 
practices and factors in the classroom.
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, results from the teacher questionnaire and the school questionnaire 
are presented with regard to students. This means that each result is either reported as the percentage of 
students who attended schools that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were 
taught by teachers who responded in a particular way. Please see the Reader’s guide for more information.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 6 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors. 
6.2	
Science teachers’ qualifications
Bachelor’s and postgraduate degrees
Table 6.1 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students according to their science teachers’ 
higher education qualification levels. The table shows results for Australia and compares these with the 
international averages.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 78% were taught science by teachers who had completed a bachelor’s degree or equivalent but not a 
postgraduate degree compared to the international average of 58%.
	
z 21% had science teachers with a postgraduate university degree (i.e. doctorate, master’s or other 
postgraduate degree) compared to the international average of 34%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 62% were taught science by teachers who had completed a bachelor’s degree or equivalent compared to 
the international average of 56%.
	
z 38% of students had science teachers with a postgraduate university degree compared to the international 
average of 41%.

Geolocation differences
Figure 6.3 shows the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students according to the type of major or

schools in major cities. A significantly higher proportion of students from schools in major cities were taught 
science by teachers with a major in science education but not science compared to students in schools in 
regional and remote areas.
