Science teachers’ years of experience 
The teacher questionnaire collected information on teachers’ years of experience in the teaching profession. 
Table 6.2 presents the percentages of students in Year 4 and Year 8 according to how many years of 
experience their science teacher had. The table shows the results for Australia and the international averages.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 24% were taught by science teachers with 20 years or more experience compared to the international 
average of 43%.
	
z 29% were taught by science teachers with at least 10 but less than 20 years, which was similar to the 
international average of 28%.
	
z 25% were taught by science teachers with at least 5 but less than 10 years compared to the international 
average of 16%.
	
z 22% were taught by science teachers with less than 5 years compared to the international average of 14%. 
On average, Australian Year 4 science teachers had 13 years of experience, compared to the international 
average of 17 years.
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 26% were taught by science teachers with 20 years or more experience compared to the international 
average of 35%.
	
z 22% were taught by science teachers with at least 10 but less than 20 years compared to the international 
average of 32%.
	
z 20% were taught by science teachers with at least 5 but less than 10 years compared to the international 
average of 16%.
	
z 32% were taught by science teachers with less than 5 years experience compared to the international 
average of 17%.
On average, Australian Year 8 science teachers had 12 years of experience, compared to the international 
average of 16 years.

Table 6.4 shows the percentages of students in Year 4 and Year 8 according to the experience of their

6.4	
Science teachers’ participation in professional development 
Many education systems, including Australia’s, require registered teachers to participate in ongoing 
professional development (PD) – supplementary to their initial qualifications – to ensure that students 
benefit from up-to-date instruction methods and information. This was investigated in the teacher 
questionnaire. 
Figure 6.4 presents the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students whose science teachers reported 
participating in various forms of PD in the past 2 years. Teachers’ attendance at PD is influenced by multiple 
factors such as the availability of courses to teachers and schools, school funding, school or systemic 
requirements as well as opportunity or time and teacher interest.
From the types of professional development listed in the teacher questionnaire, Australian Year 4 and Year 8 
students were taught by science teachers who reported participation in these PD topics in the past 2 years.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 35% addressing individual students’ needs
	
z 29% improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills
	
z 19% science pedagogy/instruction
	
z 17% integrating science with other subjects
	
z 17% science curriculum
	
z 16% science content
	
z 15% integrating technology into science instruction
	
z 13% integrating environmentalism and sustainability into science instruction
	
z 9% science assessment.
Internationally, on average, the proportions of students who had science teachers who attended PD on any 
particular topic were significantly higher than Australia except improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry 
skills and addressing individual student needs, which were not significantly different to Australia. The largest 
difference in the percentage of students who had science teachers who attended PD on any particular topic 
was for science assessment, closely followed by integrating technology into science instruction and science 
content, where the international average was about 20 percentage points higher than in Australia.

80
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 64% addressing individual students’ needs
	
z 59% science curriculum
	
z 56% science content
	
z 55% science pedagogy/instruction
	
z 47% improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills
	
z 46% integrating technology into science instruction
	
z 42% science assessment
	
z 23% integrating environmentalism and sustainability into science instruction.
Internationally, on average, the proportions of students had science teachers who attended PD on any 
particular topic were similar to Australia except science curriculum and addressing individual student needs, 
which were significantly higher in Australia, and integrating environmentalism and sustainability in science 
instruction, which was higher for the international average (by a substantial 39 percentage points).

Science teachers’ areas for future professional development
FIGURE 6.5 Percentages of students by science teachers’ needs for future professional development, Australia and

The format of the teacher questionnaire allows teachers to respond freely to both questions, assessing 
teachers’ attended-PD as well future-PD needs. This means that a teacher could have responded that they 
attended PD on a particular topic in the last 2 years and then also responded that they wanted to do further 
training on this same topic in the future.

82
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
The future PD topics, for students taught by Year 4 science teachers, in order of most to least desired were:
	
z 76% integrating technology into science instruction
	
z 70% improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills
	
z 68% science assessment
	
z 67% science pedagogy/instruction
	
z 66% integrating environmentalism and sustainability into science instruction
	
z 65% integrating science with other subjects
	
z 64% science curriculum
	
z 64% science content
	
z 59% addressing individual students’ needs.
Internationally, the percentages of students whose science teachers reported needing PD were significantly 
lower than in Australia, except addressing individual students’ needs, integrating science with other subjects 
and integrating environmentalism and sustainability into science instruction which were similar to Australia. 
The percentages of Australian Year 4 students who were taught by teachers who reported needing PD were 
substantially higher across all topics when compared to the percentage that participated in PD on each topic 
over the past two years. This was the most notable for integrating technology into science instruction (a 61 
percentage-point difference) and science assessment (a 59 percentage-point difference). 
The future PD topics, for students taught by Year 8 science teachers, in order of most to least desired were:
	
z 75% improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills
	
z 66% addressing individual students’ needs
	
z 64% science assessment
	
z 63% integrating environmentalism and sustainability into science instruction
	
z 59% integrating technology into science instruction
	
z 59% science pedagogy/instruction
	
z 53% science curriculum
	
z 46% science content. 
Internationally, the percentages of students whose science teachers reported needing PD were not 
significantly different to Australia, except improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills and science 
assessment which were significantly higher in Australia. 
For some topics, there was a notably higher percentage of Australian Year 8 students who were taught by 
teachers who reported needing PD on that topic compared to the percentage of students being taught by 
teachers who participated in that topic over the past two years. The greatest differences were for the topics 
of integrating environmentalism and sustainability in science instruction (a 40 percentage-point difference) 
and improving students’ critical thinking or inquiry skills (a 28 percentage-point difference). 

Chapter 6 Science teacher preparation, professional development and job satisfaction
83
6.6	
Science teachers’ job satisfaction
Teachers’ satisfaction with their careers can impact their wellbeing and students’ attitudes towards learning, 
the classroom and their achievement. Job satisfaction can also be associated with teacher efficacy and 
therefore is an area of interest when investigating teacher effectiveness.
Teachers were asked to indicate how often (very often, often, sometimes or never or almost never) they 
agreed with the following statements:
	
z I am content with my profession as a teacher.
	
z I find my work full of meaning and purpose.
	
z I am enthusiastic about my job.
	
z My work inspires me.
	
z I am proud of the work I do.
	
z I enjoy the challenges of teaching. 
Their responses to these 6 items were combined to create the Teacher job satisfaction scale. The categories 
are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups based on their teacher’s 
scale scores: 
	
z on average, very satisfied science teachers corresponds to teachers who responded very often to 3 of the 
6 statements and often to the other 3 statements
	
z on average, less than satisfied science teachers corresponds to teachers who responded sometimes to 
3 of the 6 statements and often to the other 3 statements 
	
z all other students had somewhat satisfied teachers.
Figure 6.6 presents the proportions of students in Year 4 and Year 8 for each of the job satisfaction 
categories, along with their average science achievement scores.

Science teachers’ job satisfaction and school socioeconomic composition

At Year 4, across each level of satisfaction and for each school socioeconomic composition, the proportions 
of students with teachers who reported that they were less than, somewhat or very satisfied with their 
teaching careers, were generally similar. Only the difference between students in more affluent schools and 
neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools in terms of the proportion of students who had a 
teacher who was less than satisfied was significant.
Similarly, at Year 8, across each level of satisfaction and for each socioeconomic group, the percentages of 
students with teachers who reported that they were less than, somewhat or very satisfied with their teaching 
careers, were generally similar, with no significant differences.

CHAPTER 7
TEACHING SCIENCE
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 7 for detailed analysis.
	
z 68% of Year 4 students and 60% of Year 8 students had been taught all of the science topics before or 
during the relevant year level, compared to the international averages of 65% and 61% (respectively).
	
z 64% of Year 4 students and 38% of Year 8 students reported high clarity of instruction in their science 
lessons.
	
z At Year 4, there was no significant differences in science achievement according to the clarity of 
instruction in science lessons. However, at Year 8, students who reported high clarity of instruction 
in their science lessons had higher achievement in science than students who reported low clarity of 
instruction.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, over 70% of students had teachers who placed a lot of emphasis on 
encouraging students to ask questions about scientific phenomena and having students predict the 
outcomes of experiments of investigations, the two most popular scientific inquiry practices.
	
z 58% of Year 4 students and 84% of Year 8 students conducted experiments in their science lessons at 
least once a month.
	
z Approximately 20% of Year 4 students and Year 8 students reported disorderly behaviour in most 
science lessons. Students who reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons had higher science 
achievement than students who reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons.
	
z There was a higher percentage of Year 4 and Year 8 students who attended more disadvantaged 
schools than more affluent schools who reported disorderly behaviour in most science lessons.
	
z Around 18% of Year 4 and 15% of Year 8 students were taught science by teachers who reported that 
their teaching was limited very little by students not ready for instruction. Around 73% of students were 
taught by science teachers who reported that their teaching was limited some. Students whose science 
teachers reported that their teaching was limited very little had higher achievement than students 
whose science teachers reported that their teaching was limited a lot.

Chapter 7 Teaching science
87
7.1	
Introduction
Chapter 6 presented data on Australian students’ Year 4 and Year 8 science teachers in terms of their 
characteristics and experiences. This chapter focuses on the teaching practices and factors in the 
classroom that can support or obstruct effective science teaching as collected in the TIMSS 2023 student 
questionnaire and the teacher questionnaire. Together, Chapters 6 and 7 present information on factors 
relating to science teacher effectiveness for Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students. 
As TIMSS focuses on student outcomes, results from the teacher questionnaire and the school questionnaire 
are presented with regard to students. This means that each result is either reported as the percentage of 
students who attended a school that had certain characteristics or as the percentage of students who were 
taught by teachers who responded in a particular way. Please see the Reader’s guide for more information.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 7 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors. 
At Year 8, comparisons with other countries and the international average only include countries that study 
integrated science (not science as separate subjects such as biology, chemistry or physics).
7.2	
Instructional time in science
Instructional time in science classes is likely to impact on students’ science learning. Using TIMSS data 
derived from teachers’ reports of weekly instructional time for science and principals’ reports of how many 
days the school is open for instruction (weekly and yearly), it was possible to estimate of the average hours 
per year that Australian students received in science instruction.
Year 4 science instruction
The average time for Year 4 science instruction in Australia was 57 hours per year. The international average 
was 84 hours per year. The average annual hours of instruction time varied among other countries that 
participated in TIMSS 2023:
Highest hours 
	
z Saudi Arabia, 199 hours
	
z South Africa, 128 hours
	
z Oman, 121 hours
	
z United Arab Emirates, 
119 hours. 
Lowest hours
	
z New Zealand, 40 hours
	
z Romania, 39 hours
	
z Ireland, 34 hours
	
z The Netherlands, 31 hours.
Comparison countries
	
z Canada, 80 hours 
	
z England, 56 hours 
	
z Ireland, 34 hours 
	
z New Zealand, 40 hours 
	
z Singapore, 98 hours 
	
z the United States, 83 hours. 

88
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 8 science instruction
The average time for Year 8 science instruction in Australia was 126 hours per year. The international 
average was 125 hours per year. The average annual hours of instruction time varied among other countries 
that participated in TIMSS 2023:
Highest hours 
	
z Saudi Arabia, 193 hours 
	
z United Arab Emirates, 
160 hours
	
z the United States, 159 hours
	
z Oman, 156 hours
	
z Malaysia, 145 hours.
Lowest hours 
	
z Korea, 91 hours 
	
z Norway, 85 hours 
	
z Ireland, 82 hours
	
z Italy, 74 hours.  
Comparison countries
	
z England, 104 hours 
	
z Ireland, 82 hours 
	
z Singapore, 119 hours 
	
z the United States, 159 hours.
7.3	
Coverage of TIMSS science topics 
Teachers were asked whether the students in the participating TIMSS class had been taught each of the 
TIMSS topics mostly before or during the year of assessment. Table 7.1 shows the TIMSS topics for science 
at both Year 4 and Year 8 according to the content domains. Figure 7.1 shows the percentages of Year 4 
and Year 8 students who had been taught the TIMSS topics, on average, for each content domain. The figure 
shows the results for Australia and the international averages.
Year 4 Australian students
Sixty-eight per cent had been taught all of the TIMSS science topics before or during Year 4, compared to the 
international average of 65%. Across the domains:
	
z life science 81% compared to the international average of 78%
	
z earth science 67% compared to the international average of 63%
	
z physical science 59% compared to the international average of 57%.
Year 8 Australian students
Sixty per cent had been taught all of the TIMSS science topics before or during Year 8, compared to the 
international average of 61%. Across the domains:
	
z chemistry 66%, which is the same as the international average
	
z biology 64% compared to the international average of 78%
	
z earth science 62% compared to the international average of 75%
	
z physics 53% compared to the international average of 63%.

7.4	
Instructional clarity in science lessons
The degree to which teachers are clear in the way they deliver the science curriculum is likely to influence 
student learning. Students were asked to indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot, agree a little, disagree 
a little or disagree a lot) with each of the following statements about instructional clarity in their science 
lessons:
	
z My teacher makes it clear what we should learn in each lesson.
	
z My teacher is easy to understand.
	
z My teacher has clear answers to my questions.
	
z My teacher is good at explaining science.
	
z My teacher does a variety of things to help us learn.
	
z My teacher explains a topic again when we don’t understand.
	
z My teacher gives me helpful feedback on my work.
Students’ responses to these 7 items were combined to create the Instructional clarity in science lessons 
scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups 
based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, high clarity corresponds to students who responded agree a lot to 4 of the 7 statements and 
agree a little to the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, low clarity corresponds to students who responded disagree a little to 4 of the 7 statements 
and agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z all other students reported moderate clarity of instruction in their science lessons. 
Figure 7.2 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in the clarity of instruction 
categories, along with their average achievement in science.

whether they reported high, moderate or low clarity of instruction in their science lessons. 
At Year 8, Australian students who reported high clarity or moderate clarity of instruction in their science 
lessons had significantly higher science achievement than students who reported low clarity of instruction.

7.5	
Emphasis on science investigation 
Teachers were asked how much emphasis they place on science inquiry practices when teaching science to 
students in the participating TIMSS class. Teachers indicated the level of emphasis they placed on each of 
the following statements about science inquiry (a lot, some, none):
	
z encouraging students to ask questions about scientific phenomena
	
z having students predict the outcomes of experiments or investigations
	
z having students create representations (e.g., models, graphs) to explain scientific phenomena
	
z having students use scientific concepts to explain phenomena
	
z having students conduct experiments (hands-on or virtually)
	
z having students discuss variation in data from experiments or investigations (Year 8 only)
	
z having students use multiple sources of evidence to explain scientific phenomena (Year 8 only)
	
z having students argue about science questions (Year 8 only).
Figure 7.4 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students who had teachers who placed a 
lot of emphasis on science inquiry practices, along with the international averages.
Year 4 Australian students
The percentages of students whose teachers placed a lot of emphasis on each scientific inquiry practice are 
as follows, in order of highest to lowest percentage:
	
z 81% predict the outcomes of experiments or investigations 
	
z 72% ask questions about scientific phenomena
	
z 54% conduct experiments (hands-on or virtually) 
	
z 45% use scientific concepts to explain phenomena
	
z 36% create representations (e.g., models, graphs) to explain scientific phenomena.  
Internationally, the percentages of Year 4 students who were taught by teachers who placed a lot of 
emphasis on science inquiry practices were similar to Australia, except for predict the outcomes of 
experiments or investigations, where there was an 11 percentage-point difference.

Chapter 7 Teaching science
93
Year 8 Australian students
The percentages of students whose teachers placed a lot of emphasis on each scientific inquiry practice are 
as follows, in order of highest to lowest percentage:
	
z 76% ask questions about scientific phenomena
	
z 71% predict the outcomes of experiments or investigations 
	
z 59% use scientific concepts to explain phenomena
	
z 56% conduct experiments (hands-on or virtually) 
	
z 50% discuss variations in data from experiments or investigations  
	
z 41% create representations (e.g., models, graphs) to explain scientific phenomena
	
z 28% use multiple sources of evidence to explain scientific phenomena
	
z 16% argue about science questions.
Internationally, the percentages of Year 8 students who were taught by teachers who placed a lot of 
emphasis on science inquiry practices were similar to Australia, except for argue about science questions, 
where there was a 30 percentage-point difference, and use multiple sources to explain scientific phenomena 
where there was a 14 percentage-point difference.

7.6	
Experiments in science lessons
Figure 7.5 presents the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students, along with the average achievement in 
science, according to the frequency with which they conducted experiments in science lessons.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 27% reported conducting experiments at least once a week compared to the international average of 31%.
	
z 30% reported conducting experiments once or twice a month compared to the international average of 28%.
	
z 32% reported conducting experiments a few times a year compared to the international average of 25%.
	
z 10% never conducted experiments in their science lessons compared to the international average of 15%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 37% reported conducting experiments at least once a week compared to the international average of 26%.
	
z 47% reported conducting experiments once or twice a month compared to the international average 
of 38%.
	
z 13% reported conducting experiments a few times a year compared to the international average of 24%.
	
z 3% never conducted experiments in their science lessons compared to the international average of 11%.
Figure 7.5 shows the complex association between the reported frequency of experiments in science 
lessons and achievement, at both Year 4 and Year 8.
At Year 4, students who reported conducting experiments once or twice a month and students who reported 
conducting experiments a few times a year had significantly higher achievement than students who reported 
conducting experiments at least once a week and students who never conducted science experiments.
At Year 8, students who reported conducting experiments at least once a week, once or twice a month or a 
few times a year had significantly higher achievement than students who reported never conducting science 
experiments in lessons.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 7.6 presents the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students, according to the frequency with which 
they conducted experiments, by school socioeconomic composition. It shows that the percentages 
of Australian students who conducted science experiments were similar across all 3 levels of school 
socioeconomic composition. 
At Year 4, there was a smaller proportion of students in more affluent schools and in schools that 
were neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged who never conducted experiments than in more 
disadvantaged schools. In addition, there was a smaller proportion of Year 4 students in more affluent 
schools who conducted experiments a few times a year than in more disadvantaged schools.
At Year 8, there was a significant difference in the percentage of students who never conducted science 
experiments at neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools compared to those in more 
disadvantaged schools.

7.7	
Disorderly behaviour during science lessons 
Disorderly behaviour in class can disrupt student learning. Students were asked about the frequency of the 
following things taking place during their science lessons (never, some lessons, about half the lessons or 
every or almost every lesson):
	
z Students don’t listen to what the teacher says.
	
z There is too much noise for students to work well.
	
z My teacher has to wait a long time for students to be quiet.
	
z Students interrupt the teacher.
	
z Students do not follow the classroom rules.
	
z Other students’ behaviour makes it hard for me to concentrate.

96
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Students’ responses to these 6 items were combined to create the Disorderly behaviour during science 
lessons scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these 
groups based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, students who reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons corresponds to students who 
responded never to 3 of the 6 statements and some lessons to the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, students who reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons corresponds to students who 
responded in every or almost every lesson to 3 of the 6 statements and about half the lessons to the other 
3 statements 
	
z all other students reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons.
Figure 7.7 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of these categories, 
along with their average achievement in science.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 9 % reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons compared to the international average of 21%.
	
z 70% reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons compared to the international average of 59%.
	
z 22% reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons compared to the international average of 20%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 12% reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons compared to the international average of 28%. 
	
z 67% reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons compared to the international average of 56%.
	
z 20% reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons compared to the international average of 16%.
Figure 7.7 illustrates a pattern between reported disorderly behaviour in science lessons and science 
achievement. 
Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students who reported experiencing disorderly behaviour in only a few or no 
lessons had significantly higher science achievement than those reporting disorderly behaviour in some 
lessons. Both those students who reported disorderly behaviour in only a few or no lessons and in some 
lessons had higher science achievement than those who experienced disorderly behaviour in most lessons.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 7.8 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of the disorderly 
behaviour categories and by school socioeconomic composition. It shows that at both Year 4 and Year 
8, higher percentages of students reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons irrespective of the 
socioeconomic composition of their schools.
At Year 4, a significantly greater percentage of students from more disadvantaged and neither more affluent 
nor more disadvantaged schools reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons compared to students in 
more affluent schools. In contrast, a significantly greater percentage of students in more affluent schools 
reported disorderly behaviour in some lessons compared to students from more disadvantaged and neither 
more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools. A significantly greater percentage of students from more 
disadvantaged schools reported disorderly behaviour in few or no lessons compared to students in neither 
more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools.
At Year 8, a significantly greater percentage of students in more affluent schools reported disorderly 
behaviour in few lessons compared to students from more disadvantaged and neither more affluent 
nor more disadvantaged schools. In contrast, a significantly greater percentage of students from more 
disadvantaged schools reported disorderly behaviour in most lessons compared to students in more affluent 
schools.

Science teaching limited by students not ready for instruction
Teachers of the Year 4 and Year 8 classes that undertook TIMSS 2023 were asked about the extent to which 
instruction at their school was limited (a lot, some or not at all) by the following student attributes:
	
z students lacking prerequisite knowledge or skills
	
z students suffering from lack of basic nutrition
	
z students suffering from not enough sleep
	
z students absent from class
	
z disruptive students
	
z uninterested students
	
z distracted students
	
z students with mental, emotional or psychological impairment
	
z students with difficulties understanding the language of instruction.
Teachers’ responses to these 9 items were combined to create the Classroom teaching limited by students 
not ready for instruction scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were 
assigned to these groups based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, students with science teachers who felt their teaching was limited very little corresponds to 
teachers who responded not at all to 5 of the 9 attributes and some to the other 4 attributes. 
	
z on average, students with science teachers who felt their teaching was limited a lot corresponds to 
teachers who responded a lot to 5 of the 9 attributes and some to the other 4 attributes. 
	
z all other students had science teachers who felt their teaching was limited some.
Figure 7.9 presents the percentages of Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of these categories, 
along with their average achievement in science. It should be noted that the international average for this 
scale contains all countries at Year 8, not just the countries studying integrated science.

CHAPTER 8
STUDENT ATTITUDES AND 
ASPIRATIONS
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 8 for detailed analysis.
	
z In general, students who said they liked mathematics or science, or were confident in learning these 
subjects or valued them also scored higher on average in assessments than students who did not.
	
z 68% of Year 4 students and 42% of Year 8 students reported that they very much like or somewhat like 
learning mathematics. And 77% of Year 4 students and 62% of Year 8 students reported that they very 
much like or somewhat like learning science.
	
z 67% of Year 4 students and 45% of Year 8 students reported that they were very or somewhat confident 
in mathematics; 69% of Year 4 students and 49% of Year 8 students reported that they were very or 
somewhat confident in science.
	
z Year 8 students tended to value mathematics, with 74% strongly or somewhat valuing mathematics. 
However, levels of valuing science were lower, with 63% of Year 8 students strongly or somewhat 
valuing science.
	
z In general, a greater proportion of female students than male students did not like learning mathematics 
and science, were not confident learning these subjects, and did not value them more. This did not hold 
for Year 4 students’ like of learning science or confidence in science, where there was no significant 
difference between female and male students.
	
z In general, a greater proportion of students from affluent backgrounds (that is, had many books or 
educational resources in the home) very much liked learning mathematics and science, were very 
confident learning these subjects, and strongly valued them more than students from a disadvantaged 
background (that is, had few books or educational resources in the home), while the reverse was true 
for not liking learning mathematics and science, being not confident learning these subjects, and not 
valuing them.
	
z Female students held higher ambitions than male students, with a greater percentage aiming for 
university study. Students from affluent backgrounds were far more likely to aspire to university than 
those from disadvantaged backgrounds, with the majority of those from disadvantaged backgrounds 
expecting that the completion of secondary school would be the highest education level they 
would achieve.

Chapter 8 Student attitudes and aspirations
101
8.1	
Introduction
Internationally and in Australia, there is continuing concern about declining enrolments in science, 
technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM) courses and participation in STEM careers. 
Instilling the development of positive attitudes towards mathematics and science in secondary school 
students is vital in developing confidence, enhancing student engagement, interest, and motivation to want 
to continue studying mathematics and science. This results in students more likely wanting to pursue further 
studies in these subjects and career paths that involve mathematics and science (Zhou & Shirazi, 2025).
This chapter draws on data collected in the TIMSS student questionnaire to examine students’ general 
attitudes towards mathematics and science, their self-confidence in learning these subjects and whether 
they see value in learning them. This chapter also examines Year 8 students’ aspirations in education beyond 
early secondary school.
Figures that present results in this chapter are given without standard errors. The Chapter 8 appendix 
presents the same data with standard errors.
8.2	
Students like learning mathematics
TIMSS 2023 collected data on how students felt about learning mathematics. Students were asked to 
indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; disagree a lot) with each of the 
following statements:
	
z I enjoy learning mathematics.
	
z I learn many interesting things in mathematics.
	
z I like mathematics.
	
z I like any schoolwork that involves numbers.
	
z I like to solve mathematics problems.
	
z I look forward to mathematics lessons.
	
z Mathematics is one of my favourite subjects.
Students’ responses to these 7 items were combined to create the Students like learning mathematics scale. 
The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups based 
on their scale scores:
	
z on average, very much like learning mathematics corresponds to students who responded agree a lot to 
4 of the 7 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, do not like learning mathematics corresponds to students who responded disagree a little to 4 
of the 7 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z all other students somewhat like learning mathematics. 
Figure 8.1 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of the 3 categories of the Students 
like learning mathematics scale, and their associated scores in mathematics in each category. The positive 
relationship between liking mathematics and scoring well can clearly be seen for Year 4 and Year 8 students. 
It also shows a clear deterioration in attitudes towards mathematics from Year 4 to Year 8, both in Australia 
and internationally.

102
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 35% very much like learning mathematics compared to the international average of 44%.
	
z 33% somewhat like learning mathematics compared to the international average of 32%.
	
z 32% do not like learning mathematics compared to the international average of 24%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 10% very much like learning mathematics compared to the international average of 21%.
	
z 31% somewhat like learning mathematics compared to the international average of 32%.
	
z 58% do not like learning mathematics compared to the international average of 46%. 
Figure 8.1 shows there is a clear relationship between student attitudes towards learning mathematics and 
achievement; the more a student likes maths, the higher their achievement (a correlation of around 0.20 at 
Year 4 and 0.37 at Year 8 for Australia). 
At both Year 4 and Year 8, students who very much like learning mathematics had higher average 
mathematics achievement than students who somewhat like learning mathematics. Both groups had higher 
average mathematics achievement than students with do not like learning mathematics. All differences were 
statistically significant.
In Australia, students who very much like learning mathematics scored, on average, 41 points more at Year 
4 and 86 points more at Year 8 than those who did not like learning mathematics. Internationally, the gaps 
were 32 points at Year 4 and 50 points at Year 8.

Female and male students
Figure 8.2 shows the percentages of students in each category of the Students like learning mathematics

gender. 
A significantly larger proportion of Year 4 male students reported they very much like learning mathematics 
compared to female students. Conversely, a significantly larger proportion of female students reported that 
they do not like learning mathematics compared to male students.
A significantly larger proportion of Year 8 male students reported they very much like learning mathematics 
or somewhat like learning mathematics compared to female students. Conversely, a significantly larger 
proportion of female students reported that they do not like learning mathematics compared to male students.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 8.3 shows the percentages of students in each category of the Students like learning mathematics 
scale by student-level socioeconomic background. At both year levels there were significant differences in 
liking learning mathematics.
The proportion of Year 4 students who very much like learning mathematics was higher among those with 
many books in the home compared to those with an average number or a few books in the home. Conversely, 
there was a larger proportion of students who do not like learning mathematics in the a few books category 
compared to an average number or many books in the home. 
At Year 8, there were strong socioeconomic differences. The proportion who very much like learning 
mathematics was higher among those with many resources in the home compared to some resources in 
the home or few resources in the home. There was a smaller proportion of students who somewhat like 
learning mathematics in the few resources category compared to those with some or many resources. There 
were significant differences between all 3 groups in the proportion of students who do not like learning 
mathematics.

8.3	
Students like learning science
TIMSS 2023 collected data on how students felt about learning science. Students were asked to indicate 
their level of agreement (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; disagree a lot) with each of the following 
statements:
	
z I enjoy learning science.
	
z I learn many interesting things in science.
	
z I like science.
	
z I look forward to learning science in school.
	
z Science teaches me how things in the world work.
	
z Science is one of my favourite subjects.
Students’ responses to these 6 items were combined to create the Students like learning science scale. The 
categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups based on 
their scale scores:
	
z on average, very much like learning science corresponds to students who responded agree a lot to 3 of the 
6 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, do not like learning science corresponds to students who responded disagree a little to 3 of 
the 6 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z all other students somewhat like learning science. 
Figure 8.4 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students in each category of the Students like 
learning science scale, and their associated scores in science in each category. It also shows Year 4 and 
Year 8 deterioration in attitudes towards science, both in Australia and internationally, although not as large 
as was seen for mathematics.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 44% of students very much like learning science compared to the international average of 53%.
	
z 33% somewhat like learning science compared to the international average of 29%.
	
z 23% do not like learning science compared to the international average of 18%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 24% of students very much like learning science compared to the international average of 40%.
	
z 39% somewhat like learning science compared to the international average of 34%.
	
z 38% do not like learning science compared to the international average of 26%. 
Figure 8.4 shows the relationship between student attitudes towards learning science and achievement; it 
was less strong than for mathematics, especially at Year 4; although still a positive relationship, the more a 
student likes science, the higher their achievement (a correlation of around 0.07 at Year 4 and 0.19 at Year 8 
for Australia). 
Year 4 students who very much like learning science and those who somewhat like learning science had 
higher average science achievement than students who do not like learning science. However, there was no 
statistically significant difference in achievement between very much like learning science and somewhat 
like learning science.
Year 8 students who very much like learning science had higher average science achievement than students 
who somewhat like learning science. Both groups had higher average science achievement than students 
with do not like learning science. All differences were statistically significant.

In Australia, students who very much like learning science scored, on average, 14 points more at Year 4 and 
60 points more at Year 8 than those who did not like learning science. Internationally, the gaps between 
students who very much like learning science and those who did not like learning science were 22 points at 
Year 4 and 43 points at Year 8.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 8.6 shows the percentages of students in each category of the Students like learning science scale by 
student-level socioeconomic background. At both year levels there were significant differences.
There was a larger proportion of Year 4 students who very much like learning science in the many books in

students who somewhat like learning science was higher for those with an average number of books in the

students do not like learning science. The proportion of students who somewhat like learning science was 
smaller among those with few resources in the home compared to those with some or many resources in the

8.4	
Students’ self-confidence in mathematics
TIMSS 2023 collected data about students’ attitudes about their mathematics abilities. They were asked to 
indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; disagree a lot) with each of the 
following statements:
	
z I usually do well in mathematics.
	
z Mathematics is harder for me than for many of my classmates. (reverse scored)
	
z I am just not good at maths (Year 4) / Mathematics is not one of my strengths. (Year 8) (reverse scored) 
	
z Mathematics is easy for me.
	
z I am good at working out difficult mathematics problems.
	
z I am good at explaining mathematics to others. (Year 8) 
	
z Mathematics is harder than any other subject for me. (reverse scored)
	
z Mathematics makes me confused. (reverse scored)

Chapter 8 Student attitudes and aspirations
107
Students responses to these 7 or 8 statements (Year 4 and year 8, respectively) were combined to create 
the Students’ confidence in mathematics scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all 
students were assigned to these groups based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, very confident in mathematics corresponds to Year 4 students who responded agree a lot to 
4 of the 7 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, not confident in mathematics corresponds to Year 4 students who responded disagree a little 
to 4 of the 7 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z on average, very confident in mathematics corresponds to Year 8 students who responded agree a lot to 
4 of the 8 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements 
	
z on average, not confident in mathematics corresponds to Year 8 students who responded disagree a little 
to 4 of the 8 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements
	
z all other students were somewhat confident in mathematics.
Figure 8.7 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of the categories of the Students’ 
confidence in mathematics scale and their associated scores. The positive relationship between self-
confidence in mathematics and scoring well can clearly be seen for both Year 4 and Year 8 students. It also 
shows a clear deterioration in self-confidence in mathematics from Year 4 to Year 8, both in Australia and 
internationally.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 26% were very confident in mathematics compared to the international average of 27%.
	
z 41% were somewhat confident in mathematics compared to the international average of 42%.
	
z 33% were not confident in mathematics compared to the international average of 31%. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 12% were very confident in mathematics compared to the international average of 13%.
	
z 32% were somewhat confident in mathematics, which was the same as the international average.
	
z 55% were not confident in mathematics, which was the same as the international average. 
Figure 8.7 shows there is a clear relationship between student self-confidence in mathematics and 
achievement; more confidence is associated with higher achievement (a correlation of around 0.48 at both 
Year 4 and Year 8 for Australia). 
At both Year 4 and Year 8, students who were very confident in mathematics had higher average 
achievement than students who were somewhat confident. Both groups had higher average mathematics 
achievement than students who were not confident. All differences were statistically significant.
In Australia, students who were very confident scored, on average, 114 points more at Year 4 and 134 points 
more at Year 8 than those who were not confident. Internationally, the gaps between students who were very 
confident and not confident were 87 points at Year 4 and 109 points at Year 8.

Socioeconomic differences
At Year 8, there were strong socioeconomic differences, with significant differences between all

Figure 8.9 shows the percentages of students in each category of the Students’ confidence in mathematics

self-confidence in mathematics.

many books in the home compared to those with an average number, both of which were higher than the 
proportion among those with a few books. The proportion of students who were somewhat confident in 
mathematics was larger among those with a few books or an average number compared to those with many 
books in the home. Finally, the proportions of students who were not confident in mathematics was higher 
among those with a few books in the home compared to those with an average number or many books in the 
home. 
At Year 8, there were strong socioeconomic differences, with significant differences between all 
socioeconomic groups in the proportion of students reporting that they were very confident, somewhat 
confident or not confident in mathematics.

8.5	
Students’ self-confidence in science
TIMSS 2023 collected data about students’ attitudes about their science abilities. They were asked to 
indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; disagree a lot) with each of the 
following statements:
	
z I usually do well in science.
	
z Science is harder for me than for many of my classmates. (reverse scored)
	
z I am just not good at science (Year 4)/ Science is not one of my strengths. (Year 8) (reverse scored)
	
z Science is easy for me.
	
z I am good at working out difficult science problems. (Year 8)
	
z I am good at explaining science to others. (Year 8)
	
z Science is harder than any other subject for me.
	
z Science makes me confused. (reverse scored)
Students’ responses to these 6 or 8 statements were combined to create the Students’ confidence in science 
scale. The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups 
based on their scale scores:
	
z on average, very confident in science corresponds to Year 4 students who responded agree a lot to 3 of 
the 6 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements 
	
z on average, not confident in science corresponds to Year 4 students who responded disagree a little to 
3 of the 6 statements and agree a little to the other 3 statements
	
z on average, very confident in science corresponds to Year 8 students who responded agree a lot to 4 of 
the 8 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements 
	
z on average, not confident in science corresponds to Year 8 students who responded disagree a little to 
4 of the 8 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements
	
z all other students were somewhat confident in science.
Figure 8.10 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of the categories of the Students’ 
confidence in science scale, and their associated scores. The positive relationship between self-confidence 
in science and scoring well can clearly be seen for both Year 4 and Year 8 students. It also shows a clear 
deterioration in self-confidence in science from Year 4 to Year 8, both in Australia and internationally.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 29% were very confident in science compared to the international average of 34%.
	
z 40% were somewhat confident in science compared to the international average of 35%.
	
z 31% were not confident in science, which was the same as the international average. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 11% were very confident in science compared to the international average of 17%.
	
z 37% were somewhat confident in science compared to the international average of 38%.
	
z 51% were not confident in science compared to the international average of 45%. 
Figure 8.10 shows there is a clear relationship between student self-confidence in science and achievement; 
more confidence is associated with higher achievement (a correlation of around 0.28 at Year 4 and 0.35 at 
Year 8 for Australia). 
At both Year 4 and Year 8, students who were very confident in science had higher average science 
achievement than students who were somewhat confident. Both groups had higher average science 
achievement than students who were not confident in science. All differences were statistically significant.

students who very confident in science and those who were not confident in learning science were 67 points 
at Year 4 and 90 points at Year 8.

socioeconomic groups in the proportion of students reporting that they were very confident, somewhat 
confident or not confident in science.

8.6	
Students’ valuing of mathematics
TIMSS 2023 collected data on the value that students attached to mathematics. Year 8 students were asked 
to indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; disagree a lot) with each of the 
following statements:
	
z I think learning mathematics will help me in my daily life.
	
z I need mathematics to learn other school subjects.
	
z I need to do well in mathematics to get into the university of my choice.
	
z I need to do well in mathematics to get the job I want.
	
z I would like a job that involves using mathematics.
	
z It is important to learn about mathematics to get ahead in the world.
	
z Learning mathematics will give me more job opportunities when I am an adult.
	
z It is important to do well in mathematics.

Chapter 8 Student attitudes and aspirations
113
Students responses to these 8 statements were combined to create the Students value mathematics scale. 
The categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups based 
on their scale scores.
	
z on average, strongly value mathematics corresponds to students who responded agree a lot to 4 of the 
8 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements. 
	
z on average, do not value mathematics corresponds to students who responded disagree a little to 4 of the 
8 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements.
	
z all other students were assigned to the somewhat value mathematics category.
Figure 8.13 shows the percentages of Year 8 students in each of the categories of the Students’ value 
mathematics scale, and their associated scores. The positive relationship between valuing mathematics and 
scoring well can clearly be seen for Year 8 students. 
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 29% strongly value mathematics compared to the international average of 34%.
	
z 45% somewhat value mathematics compared to the international average of 41%.
	
z 26% do not value mathematics compared to the international average of 24%. 
Figure 8.13 shows there is a clear relationship between student attitudes towards valuing mathematics 
and achievement, with more valuing associated with higher achievement (a correlation of around 0.25 for 
Australia). 
Year 8 students who strongly value mathematics had higher average achievement than students who 
somewhat value mathematics. Both groups had higher average achievement than students who do not value 
mathematics. All differences were statistically significant.
In Australia, students who strongly value mathematics scored, on average, 57 points more at Year 8 
than those who do not value mathematics. Internationally, the gap between students who strongly value 
mathematics and those who do not value mathematics was 40 points at Year 8.

TIMSS 2023 collected data on the value that students attached to science. Year 8 students were asked to

z It is important to do well in science.

categories are comprised in the following ways and all students were assigned to these groups based on 
their scale scores:
	
z on average, strongly value science (general or integrated science as is taught in Australia) corresponds to 
students who responded agree a lot to 4 of the 4 statements and agree a little to the other 4 statements

4 statements

Chapter 8 Student attitudes and aspirations
119
Female and male students
Differences between female and male students were evident. Overall, a larger proportion of female students 
than male students reported they wanted to attain an undergraduate university degree. This difference was 
significant. There were no observable differences between female and male students who aspired to attain 
a postgraduate qualification. Similarly, a smaller proportion of female students compared to male students 
wanted to complete post-secondary education but not attend university, for example, attend TAFE.
In contrast, a larger proportion of male students compared to female students reported they wanted to 
complete upper secondary education or lower. This difference was significant; however, it can plausibly be 
attributed to Year 8 students not at the stage in their education where they select subjects to lead them to 
preferred post-school destinations and career paths.
Socioeconomic differences
There were observable differences in aspirations by socioeconomic background. A significantly larger 
proportion of students from an affluent background (i.e. those who had many educational resources in the 
home) expected to attend university than students from a disadvantaged background (i.e. those who had 
few educational resources in the home): 
	
z 66% of students from an affluent background expected to attend university
	
z 21% of students from a disadvantaged background expected to attend university.
In contrast, a significantly larger proportion of students from a disadvantaged background did not expect to 
go on to tertiary education than students from an affluent background:
	
z 58% of students from a disadvantaged background did not expect to go on to tertiary education
	
z 18% of students from an affluent background did not expect to go on to tertiary education. 

120
CHAPTER 9
STUDENTS’ USE OF TECHNOLOGY
KEY FINDINGS
All key findings relate to Australian Year 4 and Year 8 students, except when comparisons to the 
international averages are being made. Refer to Chapter 9 for detailed analysis.
	
z 46% of Year 4 students and 74% of Year 8 students had high digital self-efficacy; a further 45% of Year 4 
students and 24% of Year 8 students had medium digital self-efficacy.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, there was a strong relationship between digital self-efficacy and mathematics 
and science achievement. Students with high digital self-efficacy had higher mathematics and science 
achievement than those with medium digital self-efficacy, who had higher achievement than those with 
low digital self-efficacy.
	
z There were significant differences in digital self-efficacy between female and male students at Year 4 
only; there were more male students with high or medium digital self-efficacy than female students, and 
more female students with low digital self-efficacy than male students.
	
z At both Year 4 and Year 8, a greater proportion of students from affluent backgrounds (that is, had 
many books or educational resources in the home) had high digital self-efficacy than students from a 
disadvantaged background (that is, had few books or educational resources in the home).
	
z Year 8 students most commonly used the internet to access assignments posted online by their teacher 
or access the textbook or other course materials. The least common activity was accessing learning 
games or activities related to mathematics or science.

Chapter 9 Students’ use of technology
121
9.1	
Introduction
Past cycles of TIMSS have determined that students in Australia have high levels of access to computing 
devices, both at school and at home (Thomson et al., 2021). However, students vary in their use of digital 
devices, both at home and in school (Fraillon et al., 2020). Variations in exposure and use of digital devices 
mean that not all students have the same level of knowledge of digital devices, or the same level of self-
efficacy in using them (Hatlevik et al., 2015). 
This chapter draws on data collected in the TIMSS student questionnaire and focuses on students’ self-
efficacy in the use of information technology, as well as how they use the internet for schoolwork.
Results in this chapter presented in figures (rather than tables) are given without standard errors. The same 
data are presented with standard errors in the Chapter 9 appendix.
9.2	
Students’ digital self-efficacy
TIMSS 2023 collected data on how confident students felt using digital tools. Students were asked to 
indicate their level of agreement (agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; or disagree a lot) with each of the 
following statements:
	
z I can write and edit text on a computer, tablet or smartphone.
	
z I can create school presentations using a computer, tablet or smartphone.
	
z I can create tables, charts, and graphs using a computer, tablet or smartphone.
	
z I can find information that I need online.
	
z I can tell if a website is trustworthy.
	
z I can easily do new things on computers, tablets or smartphones.
	
z I can help my friends or family members with using their computers, tablets or smartphones.
Students’ responses to these 7 statements were combined to create the Digital self-efficacy scale. The 
3 categories of the scale are comprised in the following way and all students were assigned to these based 
on their scale scores:
	
z on average, the high digital self-efficacy category corresponds to students who agree a lot with 4 of the 
statements and agree a little with the other 3 statements
	
z on average, the low digital self-efficacy category corresponds to students who disagree a little with 4 of 
the statements and agree a little with the other 3 statements 
	
z all other Year 4 and Year 8 students had medium digital self-efficacy.
Findings for Australia and other countries
Figure 9.1 shows the percentages of Year 4 and Year 8 students in each of the 3 categories of the Digital 
self-efficacy scale, and their associated scores in mathematics and science in each category. The positive 
relationship between digital self-efficacy and mathematics and science can clearly be seen for both Year 4 
and Year 8 students.
Year 4 Australian students
	
z 46% had high digital self-efficacy compared to the international average of 36%.
	
z 45% had medium digital self-efficacy compared to the international average of 49%.
	
z 9% had low digital self-efficacy compared to the international average of 15%. 

122
TIMSS 2023 Australia. Volume II: Student and school characteristics
Year 8 Australian students
	
z 74% had high digital self-efficacy compared to the international average of 56%.
	
z 24% had medium digital self-efficacy compared to the international average of 37%.
	
z 2% had low digital self-efficacy compared to the international average of 6%.
As can be seen in Figure 9.1, there is a clear relationship between students’ achievement and digital self-
efficacy, with higher digital self-efficacy associated with higher achievement (a correlation of 0.29 for Year 4 
mathematics, 0.27 for Year 4 science, 0.22 for Year 8 mathematics and 0.25 for Year 8 science). 
At both Year 4 and Year 8, students with high digital self-efficacy had higher average mathematics and 
science achievement than students with medium digital self-efficacy. Both groups had higher average 
mathematics and science achievement than students with low digital self-efficacy (although at Year 8, the 
proportion of Australian students who reported low digital self-efficacy was too low for accurate estimation 
of average achievement). 
In Australia, the gaps between students with high digital self-efficacy and those with low digital self-efficacy 
at Year 4 were about 86 points in mathematics achievement and 76 points in science achievement. 
At Year 8, the gaps between students with high digital self-efficacy and those with medium digital self-
efficacy were about 40 points in mathematics achievement and 46 points in science achievement. 
Internationally, the gaps between students with high digital self-efficacy and those with low digital self-
efficacy were around 41–44 points at Year 4 and 84–97 points at Year 8. All differences were statistically 
significant.

Socioeconomic differences
Figure 9.3 shows the percentages of students in each of the 3 categories of the Digital self-efficacy scale by 
student-level socioeconomic background. 
At both year levels there were significant differences in digital self-efficacy. The proportion of students who 
reported high digital literacy was higher among those with many books or many resources in the home 
compared to those with an average number of books or some resources in the home. The percentages in 
both these groups were higher than for those with few books or few resources in the home.
Conversely, the proportion of students who reported medium digital literacy was larger among those with 
few books or few resources compared to those with an average number of books or some resources in the 
home. The proportions in both these groups were higher than for those with many books or many resources 
in the home.
In terms of low digital literacy, at Year 4 only the difference between those with many books and those with 
an average number of books was significant. Whereas at Year 8, the proportions of students who reported 
low digital literacy was higher among those with few resources compared to those with some resources in 
the home; the proportions in both these groups were higher than for those with many resources in the home.

9.3	
Students’ use of internet for schoolwork
Year 8 students were asked to report how frequently they used the internet for different types of schoolwork. 
Students indicated how often they used the internet for these activities (at least once a week, once or twice a 
month, or never or almost never):
	
z access the textbook or other course materials
	
z access assignments posted online by the teacher
	
z collaborate with classmates on assignments or projects
	
z ask the teacher questions
	
z find information, articles, or tutorials about mathematics or science
	
z access learning games or activities related to mathematics or science.
Figure 9.4 presents the percentages of Australian Year 8 students who used the internet at least once a week 
for each activity, along with the international averages.
A greater percentage of Australian students indicated that they use the internet at least once a week for all 
activities than the international average. 
In Australia, the most common use of the internet for schoolwork was to access assignments posted 
online by the teacher; 71% of students reported that they did this at least once a week compared to the 
international average of 53%. The next most common use of the internet for schoolwork in Australia was 
accessing the textbook or other course materials; 65% of students reported that they did this at least once a 
week compared to the international average of 53%. These were also the most common uses of the internet 
internationally.
Other uses of the internet were less common; just under 50% of Australian students reported that they use 
the internet at least once a week to ask the teacher questions, collaborate with classmates on assignments 
or projects, or find information, articles, or tutorials about mathematics or science. The least common 
activity was accessing learning games or activities related to mathematics or science, reported by only 36% 
of Australian Year 8 students.

Cut-points for the TIMSS context questionnaire scales
Table A1.1 provides the cut-points that divide the TIMSS context questionnaire scales into 3 categories. Cut-
point 1 divides between the higher and medium categories and cut-point 2 divides between the medium and 
lower categories. Definitions of what each of these categories mean for each of these scales are given in the 
body of the report, at the start of the section that describes the findings for that scale.
Please refer to the TIMSS 2023 technical report (von Davier et al., 2024) for more information about the 
creation of these scales.
